# 221B Requirements

Oct 12, 2026 · Mikhail Stolpner

221B is built in seven phases, and each one ends in a review you approve before the next starts. Phase 1 is deliberately small: an agreed architecture, a test deployment, a read-only connection to one cluster, and the Nodes tab working end to end. The [design prototype](../design/Main.dc.html) is the reference for every screen.

## Roadmap

![Seven phases, each closed by your review](images/roadmap.png)

Phases 1 to 5 never change your workloads. Watson, in Phase 6, is the first part that does, and only after an approval.

## Scope

221B is a web console for Kubernetes clusters with two built-in assistants. Sherlock answers questions about a cluster. Watson runs kubectl and helm commands, but only after you approve each change.

**Who it is for:** the people who run clusters day to day: platform engineers, SREs and on-call staff.

**What it does**

- Shows each cluster live: Nodes, Fleets, Namespaces, Pods, Helm, Alerts, Services, Networks, Access and Policies.
- Connects to several clusters and switches between them from the top bar.
- Lets each cluster be connected read-only or read-write; read-only clusters refuse every change.
- Sorts every table by any column and exports it to Excel.

**What it does not do**

- Create, upgrade or delete clusters. It works with clusters that already exist.
- Show Secret values. It shows Secret keys and sizes only, to every user, everywhere.
- Store metrics or logs long term. It reads them live from the cluster and metrics-server.
- Run as a hosted multi-tenant service. Each installation runs in the owner's own Kubernetes environment.

## Architecture

221B is one Go server and a browser app, shipped as one container image. The server keeps a live, watched copy of each cluster it connects to and sends each browser only the rows its screen shows. Phase 1 runs inside the cluster it shows. Phase 3 adds a management mode, where one installation connects to other clusters with stored credentials.

| Decision | Choice | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Server language | Go | client-go, the Helm SDK, kubectl's drain and describe code and the Pod Security admission library are all Go. |
| Browser app | TypeScript and React | The prototype's screens map onto React components; table virtualization is mature. |
| Packaging | One image: the Go binary serves the built browser app | One thing to deploy, version and scan. |
| Deployment | Helm chart | The same chart installs test and production. |
| Live updates | Watch caches (informers) on the server; Server-Sent Events to the browser | Changes appear within seconds; one watch per resource type per cluster, shared by every viewer. |
| Where rows are built | On the server | Browsers never receive raw objects, so Secret values are filtered in one place and payloads stay small. |
| Cluster access, Phase 1 | In-cluster ServiceAccount with a read-only ClusterRole | No stored credentials; least privilege. |
| People's access, Phase 3 | OIDC sign-in; calls to Kubernetes made as the signed-in person (impersonation). Proposed; see Open questions | Kubernetes RBAC decides what each person may see and change, and the audit log names them. |
| Claude API, Phase 5 | Called only from the server; key kept in a Kubernetes Secret | Keys and cluster data never reach the browser. |

![Only the 221B server talks to clusters and the Claude API](images/architecture.png)

Everything a browser sees passes through the row builders, which is where Secret values are dropped.

## Requirements for every phase

These apply to every phase: a phase is done only when everything it builds meets them.

| ID | Area | Requirement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| X-01 | Secrets | Secret values are never shown, logged, exported, kept in the browser or sent to Sherlock. Screens show Secret keys and sizes only; an environment variable taken from a Secret shows the reference, not the value. (Amended in Phase 10, SE-03: where the chart turns `secrets.reveal` on, a SuperAdmin, or an Admin of the Secret's namespace, may ask for one value at a time, read live as them and written to the audit log; it still enters no cache, log, export or Sherlock prompt.) (Amended on November 9, 2026, BK-15, ADR-137, with the owner's agreement: a backup carries the secrets the console keeps of its own (its connections' credentials, tokens, API keys, channel addresses, password hashes) sealed with the install's backup key, AES-256-GCM, never in clear; the key stays in its Kubernetes Secret and is never shown, logged or exported. Secret values read from clusters are still never exported.) (Amended on November 20, 2026, DB-71, decided by the owner: a sensitive Automation variable's value is a secret the console keeps of its own: it is kept in a Kubernetes Secret, never in a table, a log line, an audit event, an error's text or an answer of the API, and a backup carries it sealed with the install's key.) |
| X-02 | Permissions | Each phase asks Kubernetes only for the resources and verbs its screens use, and the Helm chart grants exactly that. |
| X-03 | Changes | Nothing changes your workloads before Phase 6; until then 221B writes only its own settings, in its own namespace. From Phase 6, every change needs an explicit approval and is written to the audit log. |
| X-04 | Credentials | Stored credentials (tokens, client keys, kubeconfigs, the Claude API key) live in Kubernetes Secrets and are never sent back to the browser after saving. |
| X-05 | Container | Runs as non-root with a read-only root filesystem and no privilege escalation, and passes the Pod Security restricted profile. |
| X-06 | Browser | A strict Content Security Policy and no third-party scripts at runtime. From Phase 3, every request that changes anything is protected against cross-site request forgery. |
| X-07 | Freshness | A change in the cluster shows on screen within 5 seconds (95th percentile), without a reload. |
| X-08 | Scale | Phase 1: 500 nodes and 15,000 pods per cluster. Phase 7: 5,000 nodes, the upstream limit ([source](https://kubernetes.io/docs/setup/best-practices/cluster-large/)), and 150,000 pods. Sorting or filtering 10,000 rows takes under 200 ms. |
| X-09 | Footprint | Server memory stays under 1 GiB at Phase 1 scale; caches drop fields no screen uses, such as managed fields. |
| X-10 | Compatibility | Any conformant cluster on a Kubernetes minor release still maintained upstream, today 1.35 to 1.37 ([source](https://kubernetes.io/releases/)). Current Chrome, Edge, Firefox and Safari. |
| X-11 | Design | Follows the prototype: layout, light and dark themes, and the More menu that takes tabs as the window narrows. Usable at 390 px phone width. |
| X-12 | Accessibility | WCAG 2.2 AA: every menu, table and drawer works by keyboard, with visible focus and screen-reader labels. |
| X-13 | Tables | Every table sorts by any column, filters, picks columns and exports to Excel exactly the rows and columns on screen. |
| X-14 | Testing | Unit tests, integration tests against a real API server (kind) and browser end-to-end tests for every screen run on every pull request; main stays releasable. |
| X-15 | Operations | Health and readiness probes, structured JSON logs, and Prometheus metrics for requests, watch delay and cache size. |
| X-16 | Privacy | No telemetry. 221B calls out only to the clusters it shows, the sign-in provider, an optional audit webhook, and the Claude API once an admin turns Sherlock on (Phase 5). |
| X-17 | Licensing | Apache 2.0. Every dependency has a compatible license (no GPL or AGPL), checked in CI. |

## Phase 1: Foundation

Phase 1 proves the whole path on one screen. Code becomes an image, the image runs on a test cluster, the server reads that cluster read-only, and the Nodes tab works as the prototype shows it. It ends with your review of those four things; Phase 2 starts only after you approve.

### Architecture and design

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P1-01 | An architecture document in the repo: components, data flow, security model, deployment modes, and a decision record for each decision in Architecture above. | You approve it at the review. |
| P1-02 | Repository layout: `server/` (Go), `web/` (TypeScript and React), `deploy/helm/221b`, `design/` (the prototype) and `docs/`. | `make image` builds the image from a clean checkout. |
| P1-03 | The app shell follows the prototype: top bar, type, colors, light and dark themes. | Screenshots match the prototype at 1440 px and 390 px wide. |

### Build and test deployment

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P1-04 | CI on every pull request and push to main: lint, unit tests, browser build, image build, license check and vulnerability scan. | All checks pass on a pull request; a critical or high vulnerability fails the build. |
| P1-05 | One container image: the Go binary serving the built browser app, on a distroless base, non-root, read-only root filesystem. Pushed to GitHub Container Registry, private while the repo is private. | The image is under 200 MB (raised from 60 MB to 80 MB in Phase 6, for kubectl and the Helm SDK, and to 200 MB in Phase 8) and its scan is clean. |
| P1-06 | A Helm chart: Deployment, Service (ClusterIP), ServiceAccount, read-only ClusterRole and binding, NetworkPolicy, probes, resource requests and limits. No Ingress yet. | `helm install` brings the pod to Ready within 60 seconds; `helm upgrade` works and `helm uninstall` leaves nothing behind. |
| P1-07 | A test deployment on a cluster you choose, opened through `kubectl port-forward`. CI also installs the chart in a kind cluster and runs the end-to-end tests there. | You open the Nodes tab of the test cluster in your browser. |

### Kubernetes integration

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P1-08 | Connects with the in-cluster ServiceAccount when deployed, or with a kubeconfig context when run on a laptop for development. | Both modes show the same nodes. |
| P1-09 | Reads only what the tab needs: get, list and watch on nodes and pods; get and list on node and pod metrics. | `kubectl auth can-i --list` for the ServiceAccount shows nothing more, and reading a Secret is refused. |
| P1-10 | Keeps watched caches of nodes and pods that recover by themselves after a dropped watch or an API server restart. | A node cordoned with kubectl shows as cordoned within 5 seconds. After an API server restart the tab is current within 30 seconds, without a reload. |
| P1-11 | The top bar shows the cluster name (a chart value), the Kubernetes version read from the API server, and the connection status. | Status turns red within 30 seconds of losing the API server and green when it returns. |
| P1-12 | CPU and memory in use come from metrics-server when it is installed. | Without metrics-server the tab still works and marks usage as unavailable. |

### Nodes tab

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P1-13 | Columns: Node, Role (one short column), Status, Problems, CPU and Memory (requested and in use against allocatable; allocatable to Kubernetes; free to schedule), Pods (running of capacity) and Age; Taints, Zone, Labels and Kubelet available but hidden by default. *Problems left the table at the Phase 10.1 review (M-02): Status sorts problems first and the drawer lists them.* | Every value matches `kubectl describe node` on the test cluster. |
| P1-14 | The cluster summary (control-plane and worker counts, pods, cordoned nodes, nodes with problems, and totals for CPU, memory and pods) moves to a home page in a later phase; the Nodes tab shows the table only. | The Nodes tab has no summary. |
| P1-15 | Search by name, role filter (All, Control plane, Workers), sort by any column, column picker, and an Export menu (Excel, JSON) of what is on screen, next to the column picker on the right. | The exported sheet and JSON have the same rows, columns and order as the screen. |
| P1-16 | Problems come from node conditions (not ready; memory, disk or PID pressure; network unavailable), from cordoning, and from pods on the node that are not ready. | A node made not ready and a crash-looping pod on another node each show as a problem that names its cause. |
| P1-17 | A details drawer per node, in this order: status and conditions, problems, taints, labels with kubernetes.io labels hidden by default, then CPU, Memory and Pods as plain sections (allocatable, requested, free, in use, limits; pod counts), then system info (uptime when the kubelet can be asked, internal IP, zone, OS, kernel, kubelet, runtime, joined). | It opens with Enter, closes with Escape, and returns focus to the row. |
| P1-18 | Works at 500 nodes and 15,000 pods, simulated with kwok. | First screen within 2 seconds, sorting within 200 ms, server memory under 1 GiB. |
| P1-19 | A Settings button in the top bar (in place of the theme switch) opens a dialog with the theme and the refresh rate. The refresh rate applies to every open console at the same moment and is kept on the server, so the number of browsers does not add requests to the Kubernetes API. | Two browsers show the same refresh time; a rate set in one shows in the other without a reload and survives a restart of the server. |

**Not in Phase 1:** other tabs, the cluster menu and connection editing, sign-in and Ingress, Sherlock and Watson, label and taint filters, and the cordon and drain buttons in the drawer.

### Your review

- [ ] Architecture document and decision records approved (P1-01)
- [ ] Test deployment installed, upgraded and removed with the chart (P1-06, P1-07)
- [ ] Permissions and live updates checked on the test cluster (P1-09, P1-10)
- [ ] Nodes tab checked against the prototype and kubectl (P1-13 to P1-17)
- [ ] Open question on reading Secrets settled, for Phase 2
- [ ] Go-ahead for Phase 2

## Phase 2: Read-only views

Phase 2 adds the everyday read-only screens: Namespaces, Pods, Services and Helm, with the prototype's navigation and the details behind every row. Nothing in it changes a cluster.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P2-01 | Navigation as designed: Nodes, Namespaces, Pods, Helm and Services in the bar while they fit, and the More menu takes tabs from the right as the window narrows (amended at the Phase 2 review: nothing is rolled up while there is room). | From 390 px wide upward no tab is cut off, and the open tab always shows. |
| P2-02 | Namespaces tab: every namespace and what it holds, a tab per kind with its count, as the Helm tab has them (Pods first, Helm releases last), in place of a table per kind under a kinds funnel (changed at the second Toolbox review). | Counts match `kubectl get` for each kind. |
| P2-03 | Pods tab: status, restarts, node, namespace, CPU and memory against requests and limits, and age; filters by namespace, node and status. The status segments are All, Running, Not ready, Pending, Unknown (on a node that stopped reporting), Failing and Completed; a segment shows only while pods are in it, and the chosen one stays, at 0, until another is chosen. | A crash-looping pod shows its last termination reason. |
| P2-04 | Pod details: containers and their states, events, owner chain, volumes, environment, and logs (live, previous container, container picker, download). | Logs start within 2 seconds; a pod deleted while open says so. |
| P2-05 | Services tab: type, cluster IP, ports, ready endpoints, and the External columns (external IP or pending, node ports, Ingress hostname). Details list endpoints and the pods behind them. | A LoadBalancer without an external IP shows as pending, with the reason. |
| P2-06 | (Replaced by the Phase 10 Helm tab, HA-01 to HA-20, on October 28, 2026; what it showed lives on there.) Helm tab: releases with chart, app version, revision, status and last update. Details show revision history, notes and user-supplied values. | Matches `helm list -A` and `helm history` for every release. |
| P2-07 | Secret handling: the server reads Secrets only to list keys and sizes and to read Helm release records. Helm values that look like credentials are masked: by key, in `{name, value}` lists, and any value that carries a credential. A release's rendered notes are masked the same way, or replaced when they cannot be. | An automated test proves a known Secret value appears in no response, log or export. |
| P2-08 | A YAML view of every object, with Secret data replaced by key names and sizes and managed fields hidden; every object also opens its own details (facts by kind, conditions, labels, events, a workload's pods), with pods and Services opening their full drawers wherever they are listed (amended at the Phase 2 review). | The YAML matches `kubectl get -o yaml` apart from those two changes. |
| P2-09 | Links between tabs: a node to its pods, a pod to its Service and Helm release, a Service to its pods. Label and taint filters on Nodes, held back from Phase 1. | Each link opens the right tab with the right filter. |
| P2-10 | Permissions: get, list and watch on the kinds these tabs show, logs and events. A chart setting turns Secret reading off, which hides Secret keys and the Helm tab. | With the setting off, the ServiceAccount cannot read any Secret. |

**Not in Phase 2:** changes to clusters, more than one cluster, sign-in, and the Fleets, Alerts, Networks, Access and Policies tabs.

**Asked for at the Phase 1 review, built in Phase 2:** the search box fills in suggestions as you type; every table has the same Columns and Export controls, and Export offers CSV besides Excel and JSON; a node's pod count links to the Pods tab filtered to that node.

### Your review

- [ ] Navigation and the four new tabs checked against the prototype and kubectl on the test cluster (P2-01 to P2-06)
- [ ] Secret handling and the YAML view checked: keys and sizes only, Helm credentials masked, managed fields hidden (P2-07, P2-08)
- [ ] Links between tabs and the label and taint filters checked (P2-09)
- [ ] Permissions checked with Secrets on and off (P2-10)
- [ ] Go-ahead for Phase 3

**Review:** you check the four new tabs on the test cluster against the prototype and kubectl, and approve Phase 3. The guide is [phase-2-review.md](phase-2-review.md).

## Phase 3: People and clusters

Phase 3 makes 221B safe to share: people sign in, Kubernetes decides what each person may see, and one installation can show several clusters. It also adds the audit log that Phases 5 and 6 depend on.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P3-01 | Sign-in with any OIDC provider. Sessions live in Secure, HttpOnly, SameSite cookies and expire after a set idle time; a session made while the session store is away is the cookie itself, signed, and lasts `auth.outageSession` (DB-65). What the page asks by itself is not activity, so a tab left open, a wall display too, is signed out after `auth.sessionIdle`; a person's pointer and keys, and typing in a terminal, are. **Nothing is read past 64 KiB before sign-in** (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 9): the sign-in, change-password and first-run forms and the OIDC start read at most 64 KiB of a request's body, and the sign-in and first-run pages, which also answer any POST made without a session, read none of it. **A session of a way of signing in the console no longer has is refused**, a stored one as a signed one: after the sign-in mode changes (local to OIDC, say), the sessions of the old mode end at their next request (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 11). The groups claim (`auth.oidc.groupsClaim`) is read by its name first, so a namespaced claim (`https://corp.example/groups`, as Auth0 issues) works, and as a dotted path into nested claims (`realm_access.roles`) only when no claim has that name, as the user name's claim is (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 15). The provider's keys are read again (at most once a minute) when an ID token names a kid the set lacks, and also when no key that may have signed it verifies it: a key rotated under the same kid, or a provider that publishes none (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). With a shared session store a replica that finds a session idle by the last activity stored (written back every 5 minutes) refuses it, and ends it for every replica only once it is idle by more than the idle limit and the 5 minutes, as the sweep does: another replica may hold activity not written back yet (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). A sign-out on a replica that cannot look the session up (the session store away, and the replica never held it) ends it all the same: refused there, and the store told when it is back (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). | No page or API answers without a session, except the health probes. `TestABodyBeforeSignInIsReadNoFurtherThanTheFormsLimit` (an 8 MiB body to a page without a session, to the first-run page and to the OIDC start: no more than 64 KiB read). `TestASessionOfTheOldSignInModeIsRefused` (a local SuperAdmin's stored session on a console upgraded to OIDC over the same store: 401). `TestAGroupsClaimNamedWithADotIsReadByItsName` (package `auth`). `TestARotatedKeyWithTheSameKidOrNoneIsFetched` (package `auth`). `TestAnotherReplicaDoesNotEndASessionOnStaleActivity` (package `auth`). `TestASignOutWhereTheSessionCannotBeLookedUpStillEndsIt`. |
| P3-02 | Calls to Kubernetes are made as the signed-in person (user and groups, by impersonation), so Kubernetes RBAC decides what each person sees. | A person without rights to a namespace does not see its pods anywhere, including search and export. |
| P3-03 | Replaced by Phase 6.1 (RB-01 to RB-57): four roles, LDAP, local groups and an Administration area. | See Phase 6.1 below. |
| P3-04 | The cluster menu as designed: switch clusters, each shown with status, environment tag and Kubernetes version. | Switching keeps the open tab and its filters where they apply. |
| P3-05 | The connection editor as designed: name, environment and description; API server address; CA (pasted PEM, system trust, or skip verification with a warning); sign-in by service account token, kubeconfig (with context picker), OIDC or client certificate; read-only or read-write access, with the RBAC YAML to grant it. | Each sign-in method connects to a test cluster. |
| P3-06 | Test connection runs the prototype's five steps: address, TLS, sign-in, Kubernetes and permissions. | Each step's failure, produced in a test, is reported at that step with its cause. |
| P3-07 | Credentials are stored in Kubernetes Secrets in 221B's namespace and never return to the browser. With a database the rest of a connection (its name, address, sign-in method and what the credential says about itself) is a row of `clusters`, and its Secret holds the credential alone (DB-64); without one the Secret holds both. Token expiry is shown and warned about 7 days ahead. | An expired token shows on the cluster page with a Fix the connection action. |
| P3-08 | Removing a cluster asks again; for production, you type its name. Its stored credentials are deleted with it. With a database its row stays, with only its id, for what still names the cluster (DB-64). | No Secret is left behind for a removed cluster. |
| P3-09 | Management mode: one installation connects directly to other clusters' API servers; the cluster it runs in is one connection among them. | Three test clusters show in one installation. |
| P3-10 | An audit log of sign-ins and connection changes (and, later, Sherlock questions and Watson commands): who, what, when, which cluster. JSON lines on standard output, plus an optional webhook. A record's `from` is the socket's peer, or the client that a proxy listed in `auth.trustedProxies` names; nobody else's `X-Forwarded-For` counts. Every record's target and detail pass through the scrubber on the way in, for every sink, and what an older console kept is cleaned at start: no credential and no host key. Free text can only be checked for what looks like one. **Text nobody signed in chose is plain text in the record** (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 8): a user name typed at a sign-in page with a control character is refused as no user name, and recorded with its control characters replaced; a provider's error and a directory's are recorded the same way; and the audit table is given every text field as valid UTF-8 without a zero byte, which PostgreSQL would refuse, failing the queue's one statement at every flush. **Requests nobody signed in to make write a bounded number of records** (finding 10 of the same review): the OIDC callback counts against the sign-in's per-address limit (30 a minute) before it records anything or asks the provider, and a request refused for too many attempts (sign-in, change of password, first-run page, callback) is recorded once a minute for each action and what refused it (the address, the account, the hashing queue), saying that more in that minute are not recorded. | Each audited action writes exactly one record that names the person; a record that carries a credential or a host key reaches no sink with it. `TestATypedUserNameIsRecordedAsPlainText` (package `auth`), `TestPGStoreSavesTextPostgreSQLWouldRefuse` (package `audit`: a zero byte and invalid UTF-8 in every field; the flush and the events after it are saved). `TestUnauthenticatedRequestsWriteABoundedNumberOfRecords` (100 callbacks with a self-made state cookie write at most 31 records; 60 sign-ins from one address record one refusal). |
| P3-11 | The chart can publish 221B through an Ingress with TLS. | Plain HTTP redirects to HTTPS. |

**Not in Phase 3:** changes to clusters (read-write is stored now and used in Phase 6), and an agent for clusters that 221B cannot reach directly (see Open questions).

**Review:** you sign in, add two clusters with different sign-in methods, test, switch and remove one, and read the audit records. The guide is [phase-3-review.md](phase-3-review.md).

## Phase 4: Analysis views

Phase 4 adds the tabs that answer questions kubectl leaves to you: which nodes form a fleet, how a request reaches a pod, who can do what, and what blocks a change. Each analysis is a server API that Sherlock reuses in Phase 5.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P4-01 | Fleets tab: groups of nodes defined by label and taint selectors, one column per label and per taint, and totals per fleet. Admins create and edit fleets in a drawer; fleets are saved with 221B's settings. | A new node with matching labels joins its fleet within 5 seconds. |
| P4-02 | Networks tab: for each Ingress hostname, the path of a request (address, Ingress controller, TLS certificate, Ingress rule, Service, endpoints, pod), each hop with its state. | Breaking any hop on a test cluster turns that hop red and names the cause. |
| P4-03 | Access tab: roles and bindings with risk flags (cluster-admin, wildcards, reading Secrets, escalate, bind, impersonate), answers to "who can do this", and each identity's effective permissions. | Answers match `kubectl auth can-i` on 20 sampled cases. |
| P4-04 | Network policies: the policies per namespace, and a checker that answers whether pod A can connect to pod B on a port and names the policies that decide. | Answers match real connection tests on a cluster whose network plugin enforces policies. |
| P4-05 | Drains: for each node, whether it can be drained now and what blocks it (disruption budgets, unmanaged pods, local storage), using kubectl's drain logic. | Matches a server-side dry-run drain of each node. |
| P4-06 | Quotas (used against hard, per namespace), Pod Security levels per namespace with the pods a stricter level would reject, admission webhooks (failure policy, timeout, reachability) and priority classes. | Pod Security results match the API server's warnings for a dry-run label change. |
| P4-07 | Alerts tab: pods failing to start, pending (after a grace period, 2 minutes by default and set on the Settings page, so starting pods raise none) and recently recovered, node problems and warning events, grouped by object with counts and first and last seen. What it has shows as banners at the top of every other tab once Administration › Settings › General turns them on for everyone (off by default since 0.13, the owner, November 22, 2026); the Alerts tab's name still turns red or amber. | Each injected fault (image pull error, unschedulable pod, out-of-memory kill) appears within 30 seconds. |

**Not in Phase 4:** acting on findings. Fixes appear as the commands Watson will run in Phase 6.

**Review:** you check each tab against kubectl on a test cluster with prepared faults. The guide is [phase-4-review.md](phase-4-review.md).

## Phase 5: Sherlock

Phase 5 adds Sherlock, which answers questions about a cluster from live data. It reads only what the person asking may read, never sees Secret values, and never changes anything.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P5-01 | The panel as designed: docked at the bottom of every tab, resizable, with maximize and minimize to an icon. Ask Sherlock buttons in drawers and callouts start a question. | Behaves as the prototype on desktop and phone widths. |
| P5-02 | Sherlock answers through read-only server tools (get, list, events, recent logs, metrics and the Phase 4 analyses), run as the person asking. | A person without access to a namespace learns nothing about it through Sherlock. |
| P5-03 | Secret values are never sent to the Claude API, and log excerpts are scrubbed of tokens, keys and passwords first. | A test with planted values finds none in the captured API requests. |
| P5-04 | Answers stream, cite the objects they rely on as links into the console, and can be stopped. | 90% of answers start within 5 seconds, and every cited link opens its object. |
| P5-05 | Admin settings: Sherlock on or off per cluster, the Claude API key (kept in a Kubernetes Secret), the model, and daily and monthly spending caps. Each question is audited with its tools and token use. | Reaching a cap stops new questions with a clear message. |
| P5-06 | An evaluation set built from the prototype's sample problems, such as a pending pod, a failed Helm upgrade, a drain blocked by a budget and a LoadBalancer without an IP. | Sherlock finds the cause in at least 9 of 10 cases, measured nightly. |
| P5-07 | When a fix needs a change, Sherlock proposes the command and hands it to Watson in Phase 6. | Sherlock's toolset contains no tool that writes to a cluster or to settings shared with others. Its UI tools change only the asking person's own view (reworded by P6U-16). |
| P5-08 | Where the browser allows it, the Sherlock panel moves to a window of its own, for example on another monitor, and back. The conversation carries on in it, and Ask Sherlock buttons in the main window send their questions there. | A question asked from the main window is answered in the separate window, and closing that window brings the panel back with the conversation. |

**Not in Phase 5:** running any command. Watson arrives in Phase 6.

**Review:** you ask Sherlock about the evaluation problems on a test cluster and read the audit records and spend.

## Phase 6: Watson

Phase 6 adds Watson, the first part of 221B that changes clusters. Reads run at once; every change waits for an approval card, runs as the person who approved it, and is audited.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P6-01 | The Watson panel as designed: a terminal-style prompt per cluster beside Sherlock, output printed the way kubectl and helm print it, and live progress for long operations such as drains. | Output of each supported read command matches the real CLI. |
| P6-02 | Supported reads: kubectl get, describe, logs, top, events and auth can-i; helm list, status, history and get values. Supported changes: kubectl cordon, uncordon, drain, scale, rollout restart, rollout undo, delete pod, label and annotate; helm rollback. Anything else is refused with a clear message. | Each supported command has a passing test on kind. |
| P6-03 | The server parses commands and runs them through client-go, kubectl's own packages and the Helm SDK. It never starts a shell. | Shell characters in arguments have no effect, shown by tests. |
| P6-04 | Every change shows an approval card first: the exact command, the objects affected, a server-side dry run, and the impact, such as pods evicted and budgets touched. The approval lapses after 10 minutes or when those objects change. While its objects cannot be read, a card cannot be approved, and says why. | Changing an object after its card appears voids the approval; a card whose objects cannot be read is not approved. |
| P6-05 | Clusters connected read-only refuse every change; elsewhere the approver's own Kubernetes rights decide. | A refused change names the rule that refused it. |
| P6-06 | Buttons that change things, such as Cordon and Drain on a node, Rollback on a Helm release, and fixes Sherlock proposes, all go through the same card. | No button changes a cluster without a card. |
| P6-07 | An optional setting requires a second person to approve changes on production clusters. | With it on, nobody can approve their own change. |
| P6-08 | Every command, approval, refusal and result is audited. | Each step writes one record naming the person and cluster. |
| P6-09 | Where the browser allows it, the Watson panel moves to a window of its own, with Sherlock or apart from it, for example on another monitor, and back. Commands sent from the main window wait for approval there. | An approval made in the separate window runs the command, and closing the window brings the panel back with its commands. |

**Sherlock UI control (P6U-01 to P6U-20), added to Phase 6:** Sherlock reads what the asking person's console window shows and, while it answers, may change that person's own view: open a tab or an object's details, set filters, search, sort, show or hide columns and highlight a row, through a fixed list of eight actions run in that window. It never changes a cluster or what others see; an administrator can turn view changes off. The requirements, with their Done-when, are in [Sherlock UI control](sherlock-ui-control.md); its open questions were settled as proposed there.

**Not in Phase 6:** `kubectl apply`, `edit` and `exec`, and helm install or upgrade (see Open questions).

**Changed at the Phase 6 review:** the Alerts tab's count includes acknowledged alerts, which still stay off the banners at the top of every tab; the Nodes tab lists nodes with problems first until someone sorts; column choices are kept in each browser, also for people who sign in, and no longer with their account (which the Phase 4 review had asked for); the bar legends under the Nodes table and on the home page became tooltips on the bars and their headings; the Services tab lost its summary tiles; Sherlock no longer suggests questions to ask next; a Helm release no longer has Roll back and Ask Sherlock buttons (P6-06's rollback is typed in Watson); and Sherlock and Watson leave the main window together, in one window, rather than each on its own (P5-08, P6-09). Also asked at the Phase 6 review: Sherlock may use another AI provider than the Claude API (P5-05 now covers it): OpenAI or any OpenAI-compatible API, Amazon Bedrock or Google Vertex AI, each directly or through an enterprise gateway at an address the administrator gives, with its own headers, query and CA certificate; Settings is a page of its own rather than a dialog; an alert's Ask Sherlock became **Troubleshoot**, which asks for a troubleshooting report (summary, cause, evidence, impact, fix and prevention); and each cluster on the home page has **Find deficiencies**, which asks Sherlock to review how the cluster is set up and what to change first.

**Review:** you run each supported change on a test cluster, approve some, let others lapse, and check the audit log.

## Phase 6.1: Roles, users and Administration

Phase 6.1 replaces P3-03's two console roles with four fixed roles (SuperAdmin, SuperViewer, Admin, Viewer) that exist as Kubernetes ClusterRoles, bound to groups by the chart. It adds LDAP and local groups, a first-run page, and an Administration area (Roles, Permissions, Users, Settings). Nothing in 221B writes RBAC at run time. Design and reasoning: [roles-and-users.md](roles-and-users.md); decision record: ADR-59.

#### Roles and assignments

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-01 | Four predefined roles: Viewer, SuperViewer, Admin and SuperAdmin, as in the tables above. Each is a ClusterRole installed and upgraded by the chart under a fixed name (`221b-viewer`, `221b-super-viewer`, `221b-admin`, `221b-super-admin`), labelled `221b/managed: "true"` (Helm sets `app.kubernetes.io/managed-by` itself). The console offers no way to create, edit or delete a role. | `helm install` creates exactly these objects; the Roles page has no edit control. |
| RB-02 | Admin and SuperAdmin each have a **full** and a **read-only** definition. `rbac.roles.admin.template` and `rbac.roles.superAdmin.template` choose (default `full`). Only the chosen definition is installed, under the fixed name; the other does not exist on the cluster. Switching is a `helm upgrade` and changes no binding. | With `readOnly`, no object with a write verb for that role exists on the cluster; switching back restores it; bindings are untouched. |
| RB-03 | The **fully protected read-only deployment**: both templates `readOnly`. The chart refuses to render `localCluster.mode: write` with it, saying why, and the console shows "Changes are impossible by configuration" wherever it shows Watson's cards. | `helm template` fails on the contradiction; on a kind cluster every supported change, sent from every role, is refused by Kubernetes. |
| RB-04 | Roles are assigned by **LDAP group** (in values, RB-20) and, for local users, by **membership of a local group** for Admin and Viewer (RB-50) and by a **super role given to the user** in 221B (RB-05, RB-51). OIDC keeps `auth.admins` and `auth.adminGroups`, which now mean SuperAdmin; it can use the same four group lists (open question). | For each role and each source, a test signs in as such a person and sees what the role allows. |
| RB-05 | SuperAdmin and SuperViewer are held in 221B and apply to every connected cluster. When 221B calls Kubernetes as a person who holds one, it adds the reserved group to their groups, in the access reviews and in impersonated calls alike. Kubernetes still decides what the group may do. A change to who holds one takes effect within the access lifetime (2 minutes), including in open sessions. A local account's email and name are read again with its groups and super roles on every request, so an email changed away from one in the admin list (`auth.admins`) takes SuperAdmin from that person's open sessions at their next request (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 13). | A SuperViewer sees every namespace on two connected clusters after one assignment; removing a SuperAdmin hides the Administration pages and refuses their next change within 2 minutes, without their signing out. `TestAnEmailChangedAwayFromTheAdminListEndsTheRoleInOpenSessions` (package `auth`). |
| RB-06 | Admin and Viewer are bound per namespace: one RoleBinding of a **group** to the role's ClusterRole in each namespace, rendered by the chart from `namespaces` on a group in values (LDAP, local or OIDC). Nothing in the console or in Watson creates or deletes a binding (RB-52). To give a person a different set of namespaces, add a group in values and run `helm upgrade`; a group may have one member. Rights add up, as in Kubernetes: Viewer in `web` and Admin in `api` is both. A namespace named in values that does not exist makes the chart fail with that name, and so does the console's own namespace or the toolbox's: nobody is bound there. | A person bound in two namespaces sees those two and no others, in every tab, in search and in export (P3-02). |
| RB-07 | Everyone who signs in can see the nodes. A chart-created ClusterRole (read nodes only) is bound to the reserved group `221b:console-users`, which 221B adds for every signed-in person. It grants no other cluster-scoped kind. | A Viewer bound in one namespace sees the Nodes and Fleets tabs and cannot list volumes, storage classes or namespaces through the console. |
| RB-08 | For a person limited to namespaces, the Namespaces tab lists their own namespaces, worked out from the per-namespace verdicts the console already holds (ADR-31). No right to list namespaces is granted for this. | Bound in `web` and `api`, the tab lists those two. |
| RB-09 | Node totals ("requested", "in use", pods on a node) stay cluster-wide, as ADR-31 says today. Any list of pods behind a total is filtered to the person's namespaces. | A Viewer sees a node's total pod count, and only their own namespaces' pods in its list. |

#### What the roles contain

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-10 | **Viewer**: `get`, `list` and `watch` on every kind the console watches, Roles and RoleBindings included. The rule list is generated from the console's own list of watched kinds, so the two cannot drift. Secrets are included while `secrets.read` is on, and not otherwise; their values still never reach the screen (X-01). | A test adds a watched kind and fails until the role has it; with `secrets.read: false` the role has no Secret rule. |
| RB-11 | **SuperViewer**: the same rules cluster-wide, plus the cluster-scoped kinds the console shows (namespaces, volumes, storage classes, CustomResourceDefinitions, ClusterRoles, ClusterRoleBindings), and **no Secrets**. | A SuperViewer reads every page except Secrets, on a kind cluster. |
| RB-12 | **Admin**: the full definition is Viewer's rules plus the namespaced changes of P6-02. It has no right on Roles or RoleBindings, no `pods/exec`, and nothing cluster-scoped. Helm rollback needs `create` and `update` on Secrets in the namespace, because Helm keeps its releases there (open question). The read-only definition is Viewer's rules. | An Admin with the full definition approves a scale in a bound namespace and is refused a drain, a role change and any action elsewhere, each with Kubernetes' own message; with the read-only definition every change is refused. |
| RB-13 | **SuperAdmin**: the full definition is all verbs on all resources; the read-only definition is `list` and `watch` on all resources, `get` on every resource of the built-in API groups, Secrets included, except the subresources that run a command or proxy to one (`pods/exec`, `pods/attach`, `pods/portforward`, `pods/proxy`, `services/proxy`, `nodes/proxy`, which a kubelet and the API server authorize as `get`), and `get` on non-resource URLs. RBAC cannot deny, so those `get`s are listed; a custom resource is listed and watched but not read by name. SuperAdmin is also the console administrator of ADR-26: connections, settings, fleets, the Users page, LDAP refresh and assignments belong to it alone. | A non-SuperAdmin sees those controls disabled with the reason, and the endpoints refuse them. |
| RB-14 | The console's own account gains no permission from this work: it still impersonates and creates access reviews, writes only its own settings, connections and local users in its own namespace, and never needs `bind`, `escalate` or write access to RBAC or workloads. With sign-in off it keeps `localCluster.writeClusterRole` as today. | A chart test shows the console's ClusterRole and Role unchanged apart from the narrow reads of RB-36. |

#### Names sent to Kubernetes

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-15 | Each source has its own prefix on the names Kubernetes sees: local users and groups get `221b-local:`, LDAP ones get `221b-ldap:`, and OIDC ones keep using `auth.kubernetes.userPrefix` and `groupPrefix` as today. The Roles and Users pages hide the prefix when they show a name. Prefixes are chart values. | A local `alice` and an LDAP `alice` hold different rights; the Roles page shows both as `alice` with their source. A local group `ops` and an LDAP group `ops` are different subjects. |
| RB-16 | Names are matched exactly, case included. Local user names use lowercase letters, digits, `.`, `-` and `_`. | `Alice` and `alice` are two different subjects, and a local user cannot be created as `Alice`. |
| RB-17 | A name from any source that starts with `system:` or `221b:`, or that already carries a reserved prefix, is refused as a user name and dropped from a token's or directory's groups, with an audit record. | A directory group called `221b:superadmins` gives its members nothing and writes a record; a local user called `system:masters` cannot be created. |
| RB-18 | Impersonating a ServiceAccount or a UID stays impossible (ADR-31), with sign-in off too: the chart's `minimal` write role holds no impersonation. | Unchanged tests still pass; `hack/check-chart.sh`. |

#### LDAP

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-19 | LDAP is configured in `values.yaml` under `auth.ldap` (see Chart values above): server URL with LDAPS or StartTLS, an optional CA, the bind DN, user and group search bases, filters and attributes, nested groups, paging, time limits. The bind credential is a Kubernetes Secret named in values (`existingSecret`, key `bind-password`) and is never in values, logs, the audit log or any API response (X-04). | An integration test against OpenLDAP in CI signs in; a search of the console's logs, audit records and API responses never finds the credential. |
| RB-20 | `auth.ldap.roles` lists the LDAP groups of each role: `viewer`, `superViewer`, `admin` and `superAdmin`. An entry for `viewer` or `admin` may carry `namespaces`, which the chart turns into RoleBindings (RB-06). The group names are written as the directory has them; the console adds the prefix of RB-15. | A group listed under `admin` with two namespaces yields two RoleBindings and the Admin role for its members; `namespaces` under a super role fails the chart. |
| RB-21 | A person may sign in only if they belong to at least one group listed for any of the four roles. Others are refused with the same message whatever the reason (wrong password, not in a group, unknown user), and the audit record says which. A person in several groups holds each role; the highest is shown, and rights add up. | A valid LDAP user in no listed group cannot sign in and nothing about the groups is revealed to them. |
| RB-22 | People sign in by binding as themselves. Groups are resolved from the directory, nested groups included, with paged searches. Nothing falls back to open access: when the directory cannot be reached, new sign-ins are refused with a clear message and open sessions continue until their lifetime ends. | Stopping the directory refuses a new sign-in with that message and leaves a signed-in person working. |
| RB-23 | A person's groups are looked up again in the directory whenever their access reviews expire, no more often than every 2 minutes, and again when they approve a change. | Moving a person to another group changes what they see within 2 minutes, with no new sign-in. |
| RB-24 | The Users page lists people from the listed groups: the sync reads those groups' members at start-up, at a person's first sign-in and on `syncInterval`. The list is kept in memory with the time of the last sync, counts and any errors. A person no longer in any listed group is shown as disabled, not deleted. A failed sync keeps the old list and says so. | Removing a person from the group disables them on the next sync; stopping the directory leaves the list and shows an error. |
| RB-25 | A **Refresh** button on the Users page (SuperAdmin only, shown only with LDAP) starts a sync in the background. Only one runs at a time, and the page shows it running and then its result. | Two clicks run one sync. |

#### Bootstrap

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-26 | **Local users.** While no user exists and LDAP is not configured, the console shows a first-run page that asks for the first administrator's name and password. That person becomes a SuperAdmin. The page closes for good once a user exists, is rate-limited, and is audited. There is no generated password. **A local-users file of the wrong shape stops the start** (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 12): the file (`auth.local.users`, or `auth.local.existingSecret`'s `users.yaml`) is a list of accounts or an object with a `users` list and nothing else; no accounts is written `[]`, `users: []` or `null` (as the chart writes it). Any other mapping (one account written without its leading `- `, a list under another key) and an empty file are an error, where they were read as no accounts and opened the first-run page. Each start logs how many accounts the file names, and warns when it names none. | A second visit shows the normal sign-in; more than 5 attempts a minute are refused; the audit log has the setup. `TestAMalformedLocalUsersFileIsRefused` (package `auth`). |
| RB-27 | An optional chart value, `auth.setupToken`, must be typed on the first-run page when set. The first-run page has a Setup token field with the hint "Enter auth.setupToken if specified on deployment, if not leave blank." The chart's install notes warn when the Service is reachable from outside the cluster; they do not print the token. | With a token set, a wrong or missing token is refused. |
| RB-28 | **LDAP and OIDC.** The first SuperAdmins are the groups listed under `superAdmin` (RB-20, or the OIDC lists). They hold the role from the first start and cannot be removed in the console. Existing `auth.admins` and `auth.adminGroups` keep working. In LDAP mode, local users can still be listed in values as break-glass accounts, and cannot be created in the console (open question). | A fresh install with an LDAP group under `superAdmin` gives its members SuperAdmin at first sign-in; an upgrade keeps today's administrators. |
| RB-28a | **Local accounts with LDAP sign-in.** With `auth.mode: ldap` the console's local accounts (values and the accounts Secret) still sign in, also while the directory cannot be reached. A name held by both a local account and a directory person signs in with either password: the local one first, then the directory's; a wrong local password with the directory down is a plain refusal. | An LDAP console with the directory unreachable signs `break-glass` in; `priya` signs in locally with the local password and from the directory with the directory's (`TestLDAPLocalAccountsSignInWhenTheDirectoryIsDown`). |
| RB-29 | The chart creates the chosen role definitions, the nodes ClusterRole and the three reserved group bindings on the cluster the console runs in. For every other cluster, the connection editor shows the same YAML, in the full or the read-only variant, with the RoleBindings of the groups in values, and the connection test says which of the objects are missing or differ. Until they exist, people see an empty console there, and the page says why. | A newly connected cluster with nothing applied shows the YAML and a clear message; after applying it, a SuperViewer sees the cluster. |
| RB-30 | The last SuperAdmin cannot be removed, disabled or deleted, and the console says so. | The attempt is refused and audited. |
| RB-31 | With sign-in off, everyone is an anonymous SuperAdmin as today (ADR-26) and the Administration pages other than Permissions are not shown. Changes then run as the console's own account (ADR-52), which the four roles cannot limit, so the chart refuses `auth.mode: none` with `localCluster.mode: write` when either template is `readOnly`, and says so. | `helm template` fails on that combination. |

#### Keeping Watson inside the limits

The aim: whatever Sherlock or a person types, the console and Watson can do no more than the chart allows. The approach has one hard limit, the roles, and guards that can only narrow it. The model supplies text, never identity or scope.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-32 | **The roles are the ceiling.** Every change runs as the approver, impersonated (ADR-52), so Kubernetes refuses what the approver's role does not allow. The dry run runs as the requester. Nothing in the console grants a change that the role does not. | With the read-only SuperAdmin definition, an approved card for every supported change fails with Kubernetes' message and changes nothing. |
| RB-33 | **A model never supplies identity or scope.** The user, groups, impersonation headers, cluster and the objects a change touches come from the session, the card and the server's own dry run, never from model text. No tool Sherlock has takes any of them as input, and a Sherlock proposal is only text until a person sends it to Watson (ADR-53). | A contract test fails if any tool's input schema names a user, group, header or cluster; a proposal naming another cluster or namespace is not applied to it. |
| RB-34 | **Closed grammar.** Watson parses a command into a typed form and runs only the supported ones (P6-02, P6-03). An unknown command, flag, kind or shell character is refused before anything runs. The card shows what the server worked out (objects, impact, dry run), not what the text claimed. A command that names an object that does not exist cannot be approved. | A corpus of invented and malformed commands (unsupported verbs, hallucinated names and flags, shell metacharacters, other clusters, other namespaces) is refused or fails its dry run; none reaches approval with an effect. |
| RB-35 | **A lock in values that agrees with the roles.** `localCluster.mode` and each connection's mode stay the console-side lock: `read` refuses every change before it is parsed. The chart validates it against the templates (RB-03, RB-31). | `mode: read` refuses a change with a SuperAdmin whose role would allow it; the contradictions fail the chart. |
| RB-36 | **Fail closed on drift.** At start-up, every 5 minutes and when the roles change, the console compares the four deployed ClusterRoles of each cluster with the definitions shipped in its image, which are the same files the chart installs. A difference is shown on the Permissions page and in the audit log. With `rbac.enforce: strict`, a cluster whose roles allow more than the shipped definitions refuses every change, naming the difference. The console's account gets `get` on exactly these ClusterRoles by name, nothing more. | Editing `221b-admin` on a kind cluster to add `delete nodes` raises the warning within 5 minutes; with strict on, a change on that cluster is refused. |
| RB-37 | **Impersonation limited by values.** Because every group the console may assert is listed in values (LDAP groups, local groups, OIDC role lists), the chart limits the console's right to impersonate groups to exactly those groups, with prefixes, plus the reserved groups and `system:authenticated`. The code that builds the impersonation headers cannot emit any other group, and a test proves it never emits `system:masters`. User names cannot be limited the same way, since people are dynamic; the console computes them from the session. With OIDC and role lists, a SuperAdmin through `auth.adminGroups` is asserted as the listed groups and the reserved ones only, as with LDAP: the chart does not let the console impersonate an admin group, so asserting it got 403 on everything done in the cluster as that person; the cluster rights come through `221b:superadmins` (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 14; roles review finding 13). | A request to impersonate a group outside the list is refused by the API server; a unit test fails if the header builder can emit an unlisted group. `TestAnOIDCAdminGroupIsNotAssertedToTheClusters` (package `auth`). |
| RB-38 | **A person approves, never a model or a button of the console.** No Sherlock tool, UI action (P6U-12) or script can press Approve and run; approval needs a person's own session and a protected request (X-06). Cards lapse after 10 minutes or when the objects change (P6-04, ADR-51), and the second-person rule applies on production (P6-07). | A test drives Sherlock to propose a change and to try to approve it; no change runs. |
| RB-39 | **Read-only deployments are tested as such.** On a kind cluster with both templates `readOnly` and `mode: read`, the whole supported set of changes is tried as SuperAdmin, Admin, Viewer and SuperViewer, and the cluster is compared before and after. | Every attempt is refused and the cluster's objects are byte-for-byte unchanged. |
| RB-40 | **The ceiling is tested for every role and definition.** For each role in each definition, a test runs the whole command set plus the corpus of RB-34 and checks that exactly what the role's rules allow succeeds. The expected results are generated from the same definitions as the Permissions page, so what is documented is what is enforced. | The test matrix passes on every pull request and fails when a role gains a verb the definitions do not list. |

#### The Administration area

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-41 | A separate **Administration** entry beside the cluster tabs, at `#/admin`, with the pages **Roles**, **Permissions**, **Users** and **Settings**. Permissions is shown to everyone who signs in. Roles follows Kubernetes: a person who cannot list RoleBindings sees nothing in it. Users and Settings are SuperAdmin only. | A Viewer sees Roles and Permissions, a SuperAdmin sees all four, and the other pages answer a Viewer with a clear refusal. |
| RB-42 | **Roles** lists the four roles with a description, scope and the number of holders. Clicking a role opens its details on the right, like any other page (ADR-38): its permissions, generated from the ClusterRole itself, and every bound subject. Groups, LDAP and local, appear with their namespaces as set by Helm and cannot be changed here; a local group shows its number of members and, to a SuperAdmin, an **Add member** action (RB-43). Bindings that 221B did not make, including ServiceAccount subjects and users, are read-only and marked "not managed by 221B", with a link to the Access tab for that subject; one whose user does not exist is flagged as orphaned. Super-role holders are shown to SuperAdmin only. | A binding made with `kubectl` appears, marked, with no remove button. |
| RB-43 | **Group membership** (SuperAdmin only): a local user is added to or removed from a local group on the Users page (the details pane) or from the group on the Roles page. The form shows what the person can do afterwards, role and namespaces, worked out from values, and asks for a confirmation (RB-51). LDAP people cannot be changed here: the directory owns them. | Adding a user to a group that has two namespaces shows both in the form; after confirming, the person sees them within 2 minutes. |
| RB-44 | **Permissions**: a page, read-only for everyone, that lists the complete permissions of all four roles on the cluster open, split into **Read** (`get`, `list`, `watch`) and **Write** (every other verb). Each row names the API group, the resource or subresource, the verbs, and flags what the Access tab flags: wildcards, Secrets, `escalate`, `bind`, `impersonate`, and also `pods/exec`, `pods/attach`, `pods/portforward`, `nodes/proxy` and `serviceaccounts/token`. The page names the definition in use for Admin and SuperAdmin (full or read-only), shows totals ("reads N kinds, changes 0"), compares the live roles with the shipped definitions (RB-36) and says whether they match. The table uses the same Export menu as every other table (Excel, CSV, JSON; X-13). | The page matches `kubectl get clusterrole -o yaml` for each role on the cluster; a role edited by hand shows "differs" and names the rule. |
| RB-45 | **Users** (SuperAdmin only) lists the users 221B knows: name, source (local, LDAP, OIDC), email, status, last sign-in and super roles. A details pane on the right shows the person's groups, what they can do, and for a local user the super role and the actions of RB-46. OIDC users appear after their first sign-in. The toolbar of the table holds **Create local user** (RB-46), Columns and the Export menu; the LDAP strip above it holds only the connection's state and Refresh. | An OIDC person who has never signed in is not listed; after signing in they are. |
| RB-46 | **Local users** can be created (name, password, optionally groups), disabled, enabled, reset and deleted, in `local` mode. They and their group memberships are kept in a Secret in the console's own namespace, as connections are (`connections.store`; open question). Passwords are at least 12 characters and not the user name. Hashes are never returned by the API. Users defined in the chart (`auth.local.users`, with `groups`) are shown read-only, and a group a chart user names that values do not define fails the chart. Disabling, resetting or deleting ends that person's sessions. A session made while the session store was away (DB-65) is ended at once on every replica by a disable or a delete, and by a reset only on the replicas that hold it: elsewhere it ends within `auth.outageSession` of its sign-in, 30 minutes. A signed-in local user can change their own password, and sign-in is rate-limited per user and per address, with failures audited. A replica whose copy of the accounts does not hold a session's account (it reads them every 5 seconds, so one created on another replica can be missing for that long) reads them again before it ends the session, and ends it only when the account is still missing after a read that succeeded; when they cannot be read the request is refused and the session kept (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 2). A password is checked against the account as saved, not only this replica's copy, which can be 5 seconds older than a reset made on another replica: a sign-in whose password matches the copy reads the accounts again and is refused when the saved hash is another that the password does not match, and a change of one's own password is made only over the hash the current password was checked against (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 3). When the accounts cannot be read the copy decides, as before. **An ending holds where the session store cannot be told** (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 5): a reset, a disable, a delete and End sessions note on the account, in the same write as the change, when its sessions were ended (`sessionsEnded` of the users' Secret, a map of user names to times, kept as long as a session can live; a console that does not know it keeps it), and every replica refuses a local session signed in before then at its next request, once it has read the accounts (within 5 seconds). A session the store's list did not show (it was away) is so ended all the same, and so is a session made while the store was away (DB-65) on every replica, by a reset too; the audit record says when the store could not be listed (`N sessions ended on this replica; the session store could not be listed, so the others end at their next request`). No schema step: the accounts are a Secret. A session is its account's by the account's user name alone: the sign-in's lookup by a unique email does not apply to it, so the session of an account named by its email and taken out of values does not go on as another account that has that email (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). The per-account limit of sign-in and of the change of one's own password counts a local account's attempts in one bucket, whether its user name or its email was typed (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). The limits per address count an IPv6 client by its /64, which it can change its address within at will, and an IPv4 client by its address; the limiters' bound on what they remember forgets a key at its limit last, so filling them with other keys does not let a refused one start again (the review of November 23, 2026, cut by its cap). | A reset ends open sessions; the API response never contains a hash; the tenth failure in a minute is refused and written to the audit log. `TestAReplicaThatHasNotReadANewAccountYetKeepsItsSession` (two replicas: the first administrator, created on one, stays signed in on the other and is not shown the first-run page). `TestAResetPasswordIsCheckedAsSavedOnEveryReplica` (a reset on one replica; the old password neither signs in nor changes the password on the other before it reads the accounts again). `TestSessionsEndedWhileTheStoreIsAwayEndEverywhere` (a reset and End sessions on a replica that was never shown the sessions, with the store away: both refused on the other replica once the store is back, and the audit record says the ending was partial). `TestASessionIsNotTakenUpByAnotherAccountWithItsNameAsEmail`. `TestTheAccountLimitCountsTheNameAndTheEmailTogether`. `TestTheLimiterKeepsWhatItRefusesAndCountsAnIPv6Network` (package `auth`). |
| RB-47 | The **Settings page** that main already has moves into the Administration area as its last page, at `#/admin/settings`, with its three sections unchanged: General (refresh rate), Sherlock and Watson. Under Administration the whole page is SuperAdmin only. **Theme** is personal and every person needs it, so it moves from General to the account menu in the top bar, as Light and Dark with the hint "For this browser". The gear opens the Settings page in Administration for a SuperAdmin and is hidden from everyone else. `#/settings` redirects, and Back and Escape keep working as they do now. Fleets keep their drawer on the Fleets tab. | A Viewer can change the theme from the account menu and has no gear; a SuperAdmin changes the refresh rate and Sherlock's and Watson's settings on the page; an old `#/settings` link lands on it for a SuperAdmin and is refused with a clear message for anyone else. |
| RB-48 | Sherlock's view actions cannot reach the Administration area (P6U-06, P6U-12): `view_open` does not accept it. Amended in Phase 12.1 (SR-12): `view_admin` opens its pages, only to look, and only those the person's role opens. | The tool's list of tabs excludes `admin`; `view_admin` refuses a page the role does not open. |
| RB-49 | The Access and Policies tabs stay as cluster tabs: they analyse the whole cluster's RBAC and policies, which the Roles and Permissions pages do not. Access marks 221B's four roles and their bindings so they are easy to tell apart, and links back to Roles. | The Access tab shows `221b-admin` marked as a 221B role, and its link opens the Roles page. |

#### Local groups, and no role changes at run time

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-50 | **Local groups.** `auth.local.groups` defines groups for local users: a `name`, a `role` (`viewer` or `admin`) and `namespaces`. The chart renders one RoleBinding per namespace to the role's ClusterRole, subject the group `221b-local:<name>` (RB-15). Members are local users, listed in `auth.local.users[].groups` or added in the console (RB-43). A group name that is not defined grants nothing, and in values it fails the chart. | A group with two namespaces gives its members the role in those two and nowhere else; removing a person from it removes the access within 2 minutes; an undefined group name in `auth.local.users` fails `helm template`. |
| RB-51 | **Membership and super roles are changes to 221B, not to a cluster.** Adding or removing a local user from a group, and giving or taking a super role, use a confirmation dialog and are audited. They take effect within the access lifetime (2 minutes) in open sessions, since 221B adds the person's groups, and the reserved groups of RB-05, when it asks Kubernetes. They are never a Watson card. Making a local user a SuperAdmin waits for another SuperAdmin when one could approve, and a user name or an email in the deployment's admin list (`auth.admins`), which makes a SuperAdmin at once, cannot wait: creating a user with one is refused with 409 and audited, as changing a user's email to one is, and a backup's restore leaves such an email out and says so (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 4). | A change writes one audit record naming who changed whom and which group; a Viewer who is added to an Admin group gets Admin's rights within 2 minutes without signing out. `TestANewUserInTheAdminListIsHeldToTwoPeople` (package `auth`), `TestARestoreLeavesOutAnEmailInTheAdminList` (package `api`). |
| RB-52 | **No role writes at run time.** Neither the console nor Watson creates, changes or deletes a Role, ClusterRole, RoleBinding or ClusterRoleBinding. The console's account has no verb on RBAC (RB-14), the supported Watson commands stay as P6-02 lists them, and a command that names an RBAC kind is refused as unsupported. | A chart test finds no RBAC write in the console's roles; the unsupported-command tests include a RoleBinding and are refused. |
| RB-53 | **Deleting a local user** removes them from every group and ends their sessions, and writes one record. Nothing is left in any cluster, because no binding names a user. Disabling does the same for access and keeps the user. | After deleting a user, no group lists them and the cluster has no object with their name. |

#### Audit, tables, tests and upgrade

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RB-54 | Audited, one record each: every group-membership, super-role and user change; first-run setup; refused sign-ins and why (RB-21); refused and dropped reserved names (RB-17); drift found (RB-36); LDAP sync summaries (counts and errors, never the list of people). | Each of these writes exactly one record, and none holds a credential. |
| RB-55 | The new tables sort, filter, pick columns and export exactly what is shown through the same Export menu as the other tables (X-13). The new pages meet WCAG 2.2 AA in both themes and at 390 px (X-12). | axe finds no failure; the export test passes. |
| RB-56 | Tests (X-14): on a kind cluster, one test per role and definition that signs in as such a person and checks what the console and Watson allow and refuse (RB-40); a test that the Viewer rules match the watched kinds; chart tests for the objects in RB-29, RB-14 and RB-37; browser tests for the pages, the first-run page and the cards. | The suite passes on every pull request. |
| RB-57 | Helm upgrade keeps today's administrators and local users (P7-03), and a release that has `auth.admins` keeps working. The architecture document gets a decision record that replaces the role rules of ADR-26, and the requirements table replaces P3-03. | Upgrading from the previous release keeps every existing sign-in working. |
| RB-58 | **The console's version in the account menu** (phase-13.1-ai, the owner, November 22, 2026). The account menu's last line reads `221B 0.13.0 · build 9ef9683`, and a click copies it, with a toast. The version is the chart's (the build stamps `version.Chart` from `deploy/helm/221b/Chart.yaml`, in the Makefile and the Dockerfile, beside the commit; `git describe` names the last tag until the next is made), the build the commit's first seven characters; a released build (its version the chart's version's tag, `v0.13.0`, or `0.13.0` as CI passes it) reads `221B 0.13.0` alone. `GET /api/v1/me` gives `version` and `build` to people signed in only: not before sign-in, not on the sign-in page, and not with sign-in off, where nobody is signed in (an exact build helps an attacker match known flaws). | Go `TestTheVersionIsToldToPeopleSignedInOnlyRB58` (auth), `TestTheConsolesVersionAndBuild` (version); vitest `TopBarVersion.test.tsx`. |

**Review:** you install the chart with each sign-in mode and each role definition on a test cluster, sign in as one person per role, and check the Roles and Permissions pages, Watson's refusals and the audit log against `hack/check-rbac.sh` and the kind test.

## Phase 7: Hardening and public release

Phase 7 makes 221B ready for other people's production clusters and for the public repo: full scale, safe upgrades, an outside security review, signed releases and complete docs.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P7-01 | Meets the Phase 7 scale in X-08: 5,000 nodes and 150,000 pods, simulated with kwok. | A load-test report in the repo shows every target met. |
| P7-02 | Runs with two or more replicas; anything that must run once uses leader election. The local accounts are changed with conditional writes over the version read; a write that fails for any reason, not only a conflict, leaves the replica reading the accounts again before its next use and write, so a replica never holds old accounts under a newer version, nor writes them over a newer change (the review of November 23, 2026, finding 6). | Deleting a replica costs viewers no more than a reconnect; with `replicas.warmStandby: false`, a reconnect and the new leader's first list. `TestAFailedSaveDoesNotKeepOldAccountsUnderANewVersion` (package `auth`: a save that times out while another replica disables an account and the accounts are read again; the disable stands, and the next save does not undo it). |
| P7-03 | `helm upgrade` works from each of the two previous releases, migrating stored settings. | The upgrade tests pass in CI. |
| P7-04 | An outside security review and penetration test of sign-in, impersonation, Secret handling, Sherlock and Watson. | No high or critical finding is open. |
| P7-05 | Signed images with an SBOM and build provenance for every release. From 0.13.0 a release has two images, the console's and the bundled database's (`221b-postgres`, DB-69), published, scanned, signed and attested alike under the same tags, both for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64; the console's by CI's publish job, the database's by the release workflow, which also keeps the copy of its packages, signed the same way (DB-94). | Signature verification passes on both published images and on the copy of the packages (`release.yml` for a release's database image and copy). Not yet run: no release has been made since. |
| P7-06 | An accessibility audit against WCAG 2.2 AA (X-12). | No AA failure is open. |
| P7-07 | Docs: install, upgrade, connecting clusters, sign-in providers, RBAC templates, what Sherlock sends to the Claude API, Watson's commands, and troubleshooting. | Someone new to the project installs 221B and connects a cluster using the docs alone. |
| P7-08 | Public-repo readiness: contributing guide, code of conduct, security policy for reporting vulnerabilities, issue templates, semantic versions and release notes. | You switch the repository to public. |
| P7-09 | With several replicas, only the leader reads the clusters: it lists and watches them, polls metrics-server and the kubelets, checks connection health and role drift, and serves every data request; the other replicas sign people in and forward the rest. Warm standbys (`replicas.warmStandby`, the default) keep their watches open and poll nothing; cold ones hold nothing until they lead (ADR-70). | With two replicas, the Helm release page's CPU and memory never show — while metrics-server answers; the API server sees lists, watches and metrics reads from one replica only (cold), or watches alone from the standby (warm); `hack/ha-kind.sh` loses a follower and the leader in both modes. |

**Built in Phase 7, first part:**

- **P7-03:** `hack/upgrade-kind.sh` installs each of the two releases before the build (from their tags' charts and their published images), gives it a local user, settings, a fleet, an acknowledged alert, a connection and Sherlock's key, upgrades, rolls back and upgrades again, checking that all of it comes through each time. CI's *Upgrade from earlier releases* job runs it on every pull request and release tag, and publishing waits for it. The settings and the accounts carry a format version, and fields a console does not know are kept when it saves ([docs/upgrading.md](upgrading.md)).
- **P7-08:** [CONTRIBUTING.md](../CONTRIBUTING.md), [CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md](../CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md), [SECURITY.md](../SECURITY.md) with private reporting through GitHub, bug and feature issue templates and a pull request template. Releases follow semantic versions, from 0.6.5 and 0.6.6 (Phases 6.5 and 6.6) to 0.7.0; [CHANGELOG.md](../CHANGELOG.md) has the notes, and a version tag pushes the chart to `oci://ghcr.io/ucesys/charts` and makes a GitHub release ([docs/releasing.md](releasing.md)).

**Review:** you approve the release and the switch to public.

## Phase 8: Storage

Phase 8 adds a **Storage** tab: storage classes, persistent volumes, persistent volume claims and the pods that mount them as one tree, a drawer that traces a claim from its class to its node, and three changes through Watson. [storage.md](storage.md) has the approach, the mockups and the open questions; the rows below are its requirements.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ST-01 | A **Storage** tab in the bar after Pods (moved from after Networks on October 26, 2026), with the route `storage`, rolled into the More menu like any other tab when the window narrows (P2-01). The tab has no count; the heading line reads `N volumes · X provisioned · N classes, default <name> · N claims in N namespaces` for cluster-wide roles and `N claims in <namespaces> · X requested · the namespaces your role can see` for namespace-bound roles. | The tab opens at 1440 px and 390 px wide; the heading matches `kubectl get pv,pvc,sc -A` on the test cluster. |
| ST-02 | One flat table for every role, beside a filter sidebar like the Pods tab's (P2-03), in three sections. **Filter by namespace**: every namespace the person may see, as on the Pods tab, with its claim count and, when a ResourceQuota sets `requests.storage`, a sub-line `quota 4.0Ti of 8.0Ti · 50%`; first, for cluster-wide roles, **No claim**: the Available, Released and Failed volumes. **Filter by node**: each node a row's pods run on or its volume is attached to, so an RWX claim mounted on several nodes is under each, with the role tag and cordoned flag when the person may list nodes and names only otherwise; first **Not on a node**: pending claims, unmounted and unbound volumes. **Filter by storage class**: each StorageClass with a `default` tag and its provisioner as the sub-line, for roles that may list classes, the names the claims carry otherwise; then **No class** (hand-made volumes, claims with `storageClassName: ""`) and, in red and tagged `not found`, each class name claims use that does not exist. Each section has Select all and counts that follow the other two sections, the search and Needs attention; the selection is in the URL (`ns`, `node`, `class`; `-` for none; `(no-claim)`, `(no-node)`, `(no-class)` for the special entries), and a scope line in the toolbar says it (`22 rows · all namespaces · all nodes · all classes`). Columns: Volume, Claim, Class, Status, Capacity, Used, Access, Reclaim, Mounted by, Node, Age, the others hidden by default (ST-06); column choices are kept per cluster in the browser. What the former group headers carried lives on: a class's facts in its sidebar entry and its drawer (ST-17), a namespace's quota in its sidebar entry. At 390 px the sidebar stacks above the table. | Filtering by one namespace, one node or one class, and by each of No claim, Not on a node and No class, shows only their rows; opening `#/storage?ns=analytics&node=(no-node)` restores that selection; a Viewer bound to one namespace sees only that namespace, its pods' nodes and its claims' class names in the sidebar. |
| ST-03 | Replaced by ST-02 at the October 25, 2026 review: the class group header gave way to the sidebar's class entry (name, `default` tag, provisioner) and the class drawer (ST-17), which has the reclaim policy, binding mode, expansion and totals. | See ST-02. |
