| ST-04 | Default sort: Claim ascending, by namespace and then name, so a namespace's claims sit together; rows with no claim (Released, Available and Failed volumes) after them. Every column sorts, with the Status sort putting rows that need attention first. | A test over a seeded set of rows yields that order. |
| ST-05 | A row is a volume ⇄ claim pair. Kinds of row: **bound** (volume and claim), **pending claim** (claim, `none yet` in the Volume column), **released volume** (volume, `was <namespace>/<claim>` in the Claim column, from `spec.claimRef`), **available volume** (volume, `—`), **lost claim** (claim whose volume is gone). The Status cell shows the phase as a pill and, when the row is not healthy, one short reason line under it (ST-14). | Each kind renders from the seeded rows as in the mockup. |
| ST-06 | Columns, in order: Volume (the PV name, ellipsized, full name on hover; a hand-made volume also shows its source, such as `nfs 10.0.3.5:/hdfs`), Claim (`namespace/` dimmed, then the name), Status, Capacity (the volume's `spec.capacity.storage`, or the claim's request with a `requested` note while unbound), Used (ST-08), Access (`RWO`, `ROX`, `RWX`, `RWOP`, full mode on hover), Reclaim, Mounted by, Node, Age. Hidden by default: Mode (Filesystem or Block), Source (CSI driver and handle, NFS server and path, local path), Labels, Release (the Helm release of the claim), Binding mode. Column picker and Export (Excel, JSON) as on the other tabs. | The export has the same rows, columns and order as the screen. |
| ST-07 | **Mounted by** shows the pod's name when one pod mounts the claim and `N pods` with a chevron otherwise; the chevron expands pod sub-rows: pod name, mount path, `read-write` or `read-only`, `subPath` when set, the container, the pod's status pill and node. A pod that is not Running shows its state and age under its name (`Pending, waits for the volume`; `Unschedulable for 19d`). Pod sub-rows are collapsed by default and expanded when any of the pods is not Running. | Clicking the pod name opens the pod drawer (P2-04). |
| ST-08 | **Used** is `usedBytes` of `capacityBytes` from the kubelet Summary API (`/api/v1/nodes/<node>/proxy/stats/summary`, `pods[].volume[]` matched by `pvcRef`), read by the server with its own ServiceAccount under `kubeletStats`, cached per node and refreshed with the node metrics. Shown as a bar, a percentage and the amount; `—` for a claim no running pod mounts, for Block-mode volumes and when `kubeletStats` is off. 85% and over is amber and counts as Needs attention; 95% and over is red. A person sees usage for every claim they may read; a namespace-bound role sees no more than that. | Usage within 2% of `df` inside the pod; `--set kubeletStats=false` shows `—` everywhere and the heading says why. |
| ST-09 | A **Needs attention** checkbox with a count filters to rows that are: a claim Pending with a Warning event, a claim naming a class that does not exist, a claim Lost, a volume Released or Failed, or usage at 85% or more. The first three are what the Alerts tab shows as volume alerts today; Released and usage are 221B checks, shown here and not on the Alerts tab. | The count equals the number of rows the filter leaves. |
| ST-10 | The sidebar does the filtering by namespace, node and class (ST-02). Search matches volume name, claim name, namespace, pod name, class and node; **Select by label** filters by volume or claim labels, as the Nodes tab does for nodes (P2-09). | Searching `kafka` leaves the three Kafka rows. |
| ST-11 | Replaced by ST-02 at the October 25, 2026 review: there is no By namespace view; a namespace's storage quota is its sidebar entry's sub-line, and for a namespace-bound role Class and Volume stay plain names from the claim (`spec.storageClassName`, `spec.volumeName`) that open nothing, a class name no class matches red with `not found`. | See ST-02. |
| ST-12 | Replaced by ST-02 at the October 25, 2026 review: the flat, sortable table of All volumes is now the table for every role, with the Class column after Claim; 2,000 claims still filter and sort within 200 ms (ST-25). | See ST-02. |
| ST-13 | Clicking a claim name opens the claim's drawer and a volume name the volume's (1000 px wide like the pod drawer), each linking to the other: kicker `Volume claim in <namespace> · bound to <volume>` (or `· no volume yet`), or `Volume · bound to <namespace>/<claim>` (or `· released from <namespace>/<claim>`); a bound volume's drawer shows its claim's chain, the claim name with Copy, facts (phase pill with the time in that phase when not Bound, capacity or request, access mode, volume mode and filesystem, class, the StatefulSet or other owner, the Helm release, age), and tabs Overview, Pods, Events, Claim YAML, Volume YAML (the last only when a volume exists; Volume YAML only for roles that may read volumes). | Opens with Enter, closes with Escape, returns focus to the row (P1-17). |
| ST-14 | Overview shows **the chain**, one hop per row: 1 Storage class, 2 Volume, 3 Claim, 4 Mounted by, 5 Attached to (the node, from the VolumeAttachment for CSI volumes, from `nodeAffinity` for local volumes, `N nodes` for RWX), 6 Usage. Each hop has a value, a one-line description and a state (`in place`, `Bound`, `Running`, `attached`, `room to grow`, `Pending`, `failed`, `—`). The first hop that does not hold is red with the cause in bold; the hops that wait on it are amber; hops that cannot be reached yet are grey. The intro line above the chain names the broken hop in one sentence (`The chain breaks at the volume: longhorn has tried 14 times to create one and keeps retrying.`). The diagnosis rules, also used for the Status reason line in the table: a class the claim names does not exist → `no class named <x> · nothing will provision it` (red); a `ProvisioningFailed` Warning event → `provisioning failed · <short message>` (red); the class binds on first use and no pod is scheduled → `waits for a pod · the class binds on first use` (blue, not Needs attention); the claim has no class (`""`) and no volume matches → `no volume matches (size, access mode or selector)` (amber); otherwise Pending → `waiting for the provisioner` (amber); Lost → red; volume Released → `claim deleted <age> ago · data kept (Retain)` or `· being deleted` (amber); volume Failed → red with the message; Resizing → amber. | Each rule has a unit test with a seeded claim; breaking each hop on the test cluster turns that hop red and names the cause (as P4-02 does for Networks). |
| ST-15 | Events: the claim's own events, the attach and mount events of the pods that use it (`attachMount` in alerts.go, matched by volume or claim name), and the `FailedScheduling` events of pods waiting on the claim, in one table (Reason, Message, Count, First seen, Last seen, Reported by), Warnings first; the Overview shows the last two as a short list with a `No warnings in the last N days` line. | The table matches `kubectl get events --field-selector involvedObject.name=<claim>` plus the pods' events. |
| ST-16 | Under the chain, two cards: **Lifecycle** (what the reclaim policy means for this claim, whether expansion is allowed and whether it needs a pod restart, whether a VolumeSnapshotClass exists) and **Recent events**. A pending or failed drawer shows the Events table in place of the cards and, when Sherlock is on, three suggested questions (`Which <provisioner> disks are nearly full?`, `Which Released or Available volumes could be reclaimed?`, `What does <pod> need the volume for?`). | The suggestions open Sherlock with the question filled in. |
| ST-17 | Clicking a class name opens the **class drawer** (640 px): kicker `StorageClass · cluster-scoped · provisioner <name>`, name with the `default` tag and Copy, facts (volumes, provisioned, used, claims and how many pending, age), tabs Overview, Volumes, Parameters, YAML. Overview: **How it provisions** (provisioner with a plain-language note for known ones, reclaim policy, binding mode, expansion, mount options, allowed topologies, whether default and what that means), **Parameters** (as given), **Capacity** (used of provisioned with a meter; for a replicated provisioner the on-disk multiple when the parameters say it; claims by namespace), and a warning callout when any claim in the class is failing to provision. | Every fact matches the class YAML; the Volumes tab lists the same rows as the class's group on the tab. |
| ST-18 | Actions, in the drawer footer, each a Watson change with a server dry run and approval, the production rule included: **Expand** a claim to a larger size (patch `spec.resources.requests.storage`; offered only when the class allows expansion; the dry run and a note say whether the filesystem grows online or needs a pod restart); **Delete claim** (the confirmation names the reclaim policy and its consequence; for a claim from a StatefulSet volumeClaimTemplate it says the set recreates the claim); **Remove default** / **Set as default** on a class (patch the annotation; the footer says the default is changed in two steps and that bound claims keep their class). **Snapshot** is shown disabled with `No VolumeSnapshotClass on this cluster` until snapshots are in scope. No action deletes a volume or a class, and nothing is offered to a read-only role or a read-only deployment (RB-03, RB-32). | Each action produces one audit record; on a production cluster it waits for a second admin (P6-07). |
| ST-19 | Reads: `volumeattachments` (storage.k8s.io, cluster-scoped) joins `Watched` and the chart's ClusterRole and the Super role templates; `persistentvolumes` and `storageclasses` are already there (RB-11). Namespace-bound roles need nothing new: claims, quotas, events and pods in their namespaces. `nodes/proxy` stays out of every role template (roles.go, `superGetCore`); usage comes only from the server's own read under `kubeletStats` (ST-08). Writes for ST-18 are already in the Admin templates (`patch` and `delete` on `persistentvolumeclaims`); `patch` on `storageclasses` is added to the Super Admin full template. | `hack/check-rbac.sh` passes with the new rules; a Viewer's `kubectl auth can-i --list` shows no volume, class or attachment reads. |
| ST-20 | Everything a namespace-bound person sees comes through impersonation (P3-02): no claim of another namespace in any view, search, export, drawer, Sherlock answer or usage figure; volumes without a claim and the volume and class drawers are not offered to them, and the sidebar names only their namespaces, their pods' nodes and their claims' classes. | An e2e test as a Viewer bound to one namespace finds no name from another namespace in any response. |
| ST-21 | The Alerts tab keeps its event-driven volume alerts; its volume cards gain an **Open volume** action that opens the Storage drawer. The pod drawer's Volumes tab links a claim's pill to the Storage drawer. The Namespaces list footer names the Storage tab. The alert banner's volume items open the Storage drawer. | Each link opens the right drawer with the right row selected. |
| ST-22 | Sherlock answers storage questions through two new read tools (list classes with totals; list volumes and claims with status, usage, pods and the diagnosis of ST-14), under the same impersonation and the same limits as the other tools; `get_object` already handles a claim. Watson's three storage actions run through the existing approval flow, nothing more than the chart allows (RB-03, RB-32). | `Which claims are pending and why?` answers with the ST-14 reasons; a Watson expand on a read-only deployment is refused before the dry run. |
| ST-23 | The simulated cluster (`server/internal/sim`) gains classes, volumes, claims, attachments and usage that reproduce the mockup scenario: a default replicated class, a local class that binds on first use, an NFS class, hand-made volumes, a claim failing to provision, a claim naming a missing class, a Released volume, an Available volume, a claim waiting for a pod, and one volume at 86%. | The simulated Storage tab matches the mockups row for row. |
| ST-24 | Unit tests for the sidebar's filtering, ordering and every ST-14 rule; an e2e test on the kind cluster with its local-path provisioner covering a bound claim, a claim naming a missing class and a Released volume; the role tests of RB-56 extended with the new kinds. | CI passes; the Storage screenshots are added to `docs/screenshots/`. |
| ST-25 | 2,000 claims and 50 classes: first screen within 2 seconds, filtering and sorting within 200 ms, usage refresh adds no request per claim (one Summary read per node per refresh). | Measured with kwok as for P1-18. |

**Built in Phase 8:**

- **ST-01 to ST-12:** the tab after Pods: one table, sorted by claim, beside a filter sidebar by namespace, node and storage class as on the Pods tab (revised at the October 25, 2026 review, which replaced the views by class and by namespace), the Needs attention filter, search and Select by label, Columns and Export (Excel, CSV, JSON). The server builds the rows from its caches (`server/internal/views/storage.go`) and filters them per person like every other view; the browser groups and sorts them (`web/src/lib/volumes.ts`).
- **ST-08:** used bytes come from each kubelet's Summary API, read by the console's ServiceAccount at most every 30 seconds, one request per node that runs a pod with a claim (ADR-63).
- **ST-13 to ST-18:** the claim drawer with the chain and its diagnosis, the class drawer, and Expand, Delete claim and Set or Remove default as Watson changes, each waiting on an approval card.
- **ST-19, ST-20:** VolumeAttachments are watched and read by the chart's ClusterRole and the Super roles; full Admin gains `delete` on claims. A person bound to namespaces gets claims only, through the same access reviews and impersonation as the other tabs.
- **ST-21, ST-22:** Open volume on the Alerts tab's volume cards, the claim pill on the pod drawer, the Namespaces footer and the banner's volume items open the drawer; Sherlock has `list_storage_classes` and `list_volumes`. The Needs attention filter is a superset of the Alerts tab's volume alerts (ADR-64).
- **ST-23 to ST-25:** the simulator serves the mockups' scenario; unit tests cover the sidebar's entries and filtering, ordering and every diagnosis rule; browser tests cover the table, the sidebar, the drawers and Needs attention against the simulator, and a bound claim, a claim naming a missing class, a Released volume and a namespace-bound person on kind; 2,000 claims in 50 classes build in milliseconds and sort within 200 ms.

### Your review

- [ ] The tab checked against `kubectl get pv,pvc,sc -A` on the test cluster, at 1440 px and 390 px, in both themes (ST-01 to ST-12)
- [ ] The filter sidebar: one namespace, one node, one class, No claim, Not on a node and No class each show only their rows, and a link with `ns`, `node` or `class` opens the same selection (ST-02)
- [ ] A claim's drawer checked for a bound claim, a claim failing to provision, a claim naming a missing class and a Released volume (ST-13 to ST-16)
- [ ] The class drawer and the three Watson changes approved and run on the test cluster, each with its audit record (ST-17, ST-18)
- [ ] A Viewer bound to one namespace sees nothing of another, and no volume, class or attachment (ST-19, ST-20)
- [ ] Used figures within 2% of `df` in the pod; `kubeletStats=false` shows — (ST-08)
- [ ] The links from Alerts, the pod drawer, Namespaces and the banner, and Sherlock's answer to "Which claims are pending and why?" (ST-21, ST-22)
- [ ] The open questions of [storage.md](storage.md) settled: the mockups' answers were built
- [ ] Go-ahead to release

**Review:** you check the Storage tab on the test cluster against the mockups and kubectl, and approve the release.

## Phase 9: Toolbox

Phase 9 adds the **Toolbox**: a Tools column on the pod and node tables whose menu opens a shell, runs a command, forwards a port, attaches a debug container, starts a debug pod or opens a node shell; every one a session that lives in the Dock, every one a Watson card under the existing approval and two-person rules, every one audited. [toolbox.md](toolbox.md) has the approach, the chart values, the mockups and the open questions; the rows below are its requirements.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TB-01 | A **Tools** column on the Pods and Nodes tables and on every table that lists pods (a node's pods, a Service's pods, a workload's pods): a fixed 30 px column right after the name, no heading, one icon button per row (`aria-label="Tools for pod <name>"`). The icon is muted until the row is hovered or focused; the cell is empty when the person may use no tool on that row (TB-05). Enter or `s` on a focused row opens the menu; Escape closes it and returns focus to the row. | At 1440 px and 390 px the column keeps its width and the icon is reachable with Tab; a Viewer sees empty cells; the keyboard path has a browser test. |
| TB-02 | The pod menu: a header `Tools for pod <name> in <namespace>`, then, where the pod has an error or a warning, **Troubleshoot…** (M-04), then **Shell…** (hint: the container it will use), **Port forward…** (hint: the number of ports; the dialog offers the containers' ports and starts on the first), **Debug container…**, a divider and **Delete…**, which asks to confirm and then hands `kubectl delete pod` to Watson as a card to approve (TB-39; reversed on October 28, 2026: at once before). No footer line. Nothing else: views stay in the drawer. An item the chart, the connection or the role rules out is shown disabled with its reason (`Port forward · off on this cluster`), not hidden. *Run a command* was removed at the October 26, 2026 review, and the Port forward submenu with it. | The menu renders from the seeded pods; a disabled item carries its reason as text and `aria-disabled`; Delete asks first, with focus on Cancel. |
| TB-03 | The node menu: a header `Tools for node <name> · <role>`, then, where the node has a problem, **Troubleshoot…** (M-04), then **Node shell…** (tag `root`), **Debug pod on this node…**, a divider, **Cordon** (or **Uncordon**), which runs at once, and **Drain…**, which asks to confirm first (TB-39). *Run a command on the node* was removed at the October 26, 2026 review. | Each tool opens its dialog; Cordon runs at once; Drain asks first. |
| TB-04 | The same menu opens from a **Tools** button in the pod and node drawer headers, and the Debug pod dialog from the Namespaces tab's rows (TB-30). The drawers get no Toolbox tab. | The drawer button and the row icon open an identical menu. |
| TB-05 | What a row offers is computed in the browser from what the server reports the person may do: the chart's `toolbox` values for the cluster (TB-48) and the connection's overrides, the connection's mode (read refuses every tool), the person's roles (TB-50), and for namespace-bound roles the object's namespace. The server enforces the same rules on every request, so a hand-made request fails as a hidden item would. | A test matrix of role × values × mode × env yields the expected items; a crafted POST for a hidden item is refused with the same reason. |
| TB-06 | Every tool opens a modal dialog (600–800 px): an icon; a title naming the kind and the object (`Shell in pod gateway-…`, `Debug container for pod …`, `Node shell on node …`, `Debug pod on node …`); a kicker with namespace, cluster, a `production` tag when the connection's env is production, and the identity it runs as; the tool's choices; a facts line (recorded or not, the idle limit, "Kubernetes decides what it may do, as you"); a footer with Cancel and one primary button. The primary button opens the session at once, as the person (the dialog is their approval, TB-37), and closes the dialog; where a second person must approve, it makes the Watson card instead (TB-36). | Each dialog has a browser test; on an ordinary cluster the primary button opens the session with no card. |
| TB-07 | On a production connection the dialog adds **Reason** (required, 10 to 500 characters, shown on the card and kept in the audit record) and **For how long** (15, 30 or 60 minutes, capped by `toolbox.maxDuration`); the footer names the possible approvers (the SuperAdmins other than the requester) and says the request lapses after 30 minutes unanswered. On other connections neither field appears and the primary button reads *Open shell*, *Run*, *Start forward*, *Attach and open shell*, *Start debug pod* instead of *Request …*. | The production and non-production dialogs render as designed; a reason under 10 characters is refused in the dialog. |
| TB-08 | Dialogs remember the last choices per tool and per pod or node in the browser (container, shell, profile, image, duration), never the reason. | Reopening a dialog shows the previous choices and an empty reason. |
| TB-09 | The Dock gets a **strip** above its panes: **Assistants** (Sherlock and Watson side by side, as today), **Sessions** with a count and an `N waiting` badge, a divider, then one tab per session of this person on this cluster (`sh gateway-… · main`, `pf 5432 → postgres-0`, `node worker-003`) with a state dot (live, idle, waiting for approval, ended) and a chip (`ends 10:42`, `idle 7:40`, `waiting`, `ended`), then the existing pop-out, minimize and maximize controls. A tab's × ends a live session after a confirmation and dismisses an ended one. Minimized, the dock icon carries the count of live sessions. | The strip renders every state from the seeded sessions; a session tab survives changing tabs, opening drawers and minimizing the dock. |
| TB-10 | A **terminal pane** for shells (pod, debug container, debug pod, node): xterm.js (`@xterm/xterm`, `@xterm/addon-fit`, MIT) in the Watson pane's dark style (the `--term-*` tokens); a header with the target, pills (`Approved by <name>`, `Ends <time> · N min`, `Recorded`, `<name> is watching`), Transcript (download, TB-54) and End; a bottom bar that in the last 60 seconds of idle shows `No activity for N min · the session ends in 0:58 · any key keeps it` with a Keep button, and after the end shows the reason (`Ended · 10 min idle`, `Ended by Anna K.: "migration window over"`, `Ended · time limit`, `Ended · pod gone`, `Ended · console restarted`) with Reconnect, which starts a new request. The terminal follows the pane's size. | Typing round trip under 50 ms added by the console on the test cluster; resizing the dock resizes the terminal; the warning appears at 60 s and any key cancels it. |
| TB-11 | A **session pane** for port forwards: the steps (approved, CLI connected from `<host>`, listening on `<address>`), the command to run with Copy, the live state (open and total connections, bytes each way, last traffic, idle limit, hard limit), the connections list, the idle countdown as a bar with Extend, and End and Change local port. | Matches the mockup; counters update within 2 s of traffic. |
| TB-12 | The **Sessions** pane: every live session on the cluster the person may see (their own; all of them for a SuperAdmin), with type, target, namespace, person, started, last activity, ends, approved by, and actions (Open; Watch and End for a SuperAdmin; Review for a request waiting on this person), then the sessions ended in the last hour with their record, and a link to Administration › Sessions. A SuperAdmin ending someone else's session gives a reason the owner sees in their pane. | A SuperAdmin sees all; another person only their own; an admin's End shows its reason in the owner's pane within 3 s. |
| TB-13 | **Lifetimes.** Every session has an idle limit (`toolbox.idleTimeout`, default 10 minutes; keystrokes and output count for terminals, bytes for forwards) and a hard limit (the approved duration on a two-person cluster, `toolbox.maxDuration` elsewhere, default 60 minutes). The pane warns 60 s before the idle end; the tab's chip counts down the last 10 minutes of the hard limit. Ending a session closes the exec or forward stream, deletes the pod of a node shell or the Job of a debug pod, and writes the audit record (TB-53). A session also ends when the person's sign-in session no longer stands (`sessionStands`), when the pod or node is gone, or when the server restarts (TB-14). | Unit tests for every end cause; an e2e test lets a shell idle out and sees the warning and the reason. |
| TB-14 | Sessions live in memory on the leader, like Watson's cards; their routes are leader-only and forwarded (`api/proxy.go`). After a restart or a leader change, live sessions end with `console restarted`, and a janitor deletes the toolbox pods and Jobs whose session is gone (`activeDeadlineSeconds` on each is the backstop). A browser reconnects to its live sessions after a reload or a network blip within the idle limit. | Killing the leader ends the sessions, reaps their pods within 30 s, and the records say why; a reload keeps a live shell. |
| TB-15 | **Shell** opens an exec stream (`create pods/exec`) as the person, with `remotecommand.NewFallbackExecutor` (WebSocket `v5.channel.k8s.io`, then SPDY), a TTY, stdin and a `TerminalSizeQueue`; the command is `sh -c 'command -v bash >/dev/null && exec bash \|\| exec sh'` unless a shell is chosen; the container is the dialog's choice (the first app container by default; init, sidecar and ephemeral containers listed too). The browser reaches it over a WebSocket at `…/sessions/{id}/attach` carrying bytes and resize messages; the upgrade checks `Origin` and the CSRF token and the sign-in cookie. | On kind: open a shell, run `id`, resize, end. A container with no shell shows Kubernetes' `executable file not found` message in the pane with a button that opens the Debug container dialog (TB-24). |
| TB-16 | **Run a command** runs one command without a TTY (`sh -c <command>`), captures stdout and stderr up to Watson's `MaxOutput`, and shows them in a Dock tab that ends when the command does (`finished · exit 0`, with duration). The command, with what looks like a credential removed, and the size of the output go into the audit record (TB-53); the output itself does not, since a command can print anything. **Not offered in the menus since the October 26, 2026 review**; the server keeps the tool for now. | The output tab shows exit status and duration; the record holds the command and the output's size. |
| TB-17 | **File transfer** inside a shell session: Download runs `tar cf - <path>` on a second exec and streams the archive to the browser as a download; Upload pipes a file into `tar xf - -C <dir>` (write-mode connections only, `toolbox.shell.fileTransfer`). Both are audited with path and byte count; neither is buffered in the server. | A 1 GB download streams without server memory growth; upload is refused on a read-mode connection. |
| TB-18 | Pasting into a terminal and Sherlock's suggested commands (TB-41) place text at the prompt; only the person presses Enter. | A pasted multi-line command runs only on Enter. |
| TB-19 | **Port forward** opens a port-forward stream (`create pods/portforward`) as the person and exposes it to the person's computer through a tunnel: the `221b` CLI connects a WebSocket to `…/sessions/{id}/tunnel`, listens on the local port and multiplexes TCP connections over the tunnel (one framed stream per connection: open with the pod port, data, close); the server opens one kubelet port-forward stream pair per tunnelled connection. Any program on the computer uses the local port. | `psql` through the tunnel on kind; two concurrent connections; the pane counts them. |
| TB-20 | The **`221b` CLI** (`server/cmd/221b-cli`, one static binary for macOS, Windows and Linux, published with releases): `221b login <console-url>` signs in through the browser and the console mints a CLI token bound to the person's sign-in session (kept in the OS keychain, else a 0600 file); `221b forward <cluster>/<session> [--local PORT]` connects a session the console created; `221b sessions` lists the person's live sessions. The CLI talks to the console only, never to Kubernetes. The token is revoked with the person's sessions (RB-53) and on sign-out. | The three commands work against the simulator; a revoked token is refused with a clear message. |
| TB-21 | The session is created by the card; it waits up to 5 minutes for the CLI, then ends. The pane shows the exact command to run; the CLI prints the local address; `Auto` lets the CLI choose the port and report it. | The waiting state, the connected state and the 5-minute end are tested. |
| TB-22 | Forwards idle on bytes: no traffic for the idle limit ends the forward and cuts open connections; Extend adds 10 minutes within the hard limit. The session records connections and byte counts, never the payload. | The idle bar counts down and Extend resets it; the record lists connections and bytes. |
| TB-23 | `toolbox.portForward.enabled` per connection turns the tool off with the reason on the menu item; `cliMinVersion` refuses older CLIs with an upgrade message. | The off state matches the mockup; an old CLI is refused. |
| TB-24 | **Debug container** attaches an ephemeral container (`patch pods/ephemeralcontainers`, client-go `UpdateEphemeralContainers`) as the person: an image from the chart's allowlist (TB-48), `targetContainerName` as chosen, the pod's volumes mounted at their own paths when the switch is on (no `subPath`; PVC mounts read-only on production by default), `sleep infinity`, a name `221b-<profile>-<4 hex>`, and a security context from the chosen capability profile bounded by the namespace's Pod Security level (the dialog disables the profiles the level forbids and says why). Two profiles since the October 26, 2026 review: **general** runs as the image's own user, as capable as the namespace allows (the image's capabilities that the level admits); **restricted** runs as non-root (user 65532) and can't escalate, and is the default. The *baseline* profile was removed. Then a shell session into it opens (TB-15). | On kind: attach to a distroless pod, mount its volumes, open a shell; a profile the namespace forbids is disabled in the dialog and refused by the server. |
| TB-25 | The dialog lists the ephemeral containers already in the pod (state, image, age, who attached them) with *Open a shell in it instead*, and says a debug container stays until the pod restarts and shares the pod's memory limit. | The list and the notice render from a seeded pod. |
| TB-26 | A debug container is not a session: it has no end. The shells opened into it are sessions. The Sessions pages show the container under its pod with `until the pod restarts` and the shells opened into it. | The Sessions pane shows the container and its shells as in the mockup. |
| TB-27 | Attaching a debug container is a change to the pod and voids pending Watson cards on it (P6-04). | A pending scale card on the pod lapses when the container is attached. |
| TB-28 | **Debug pod** creates a Job (`create jobs`, in the dialog's namespace; one pod, `restartPolicy: Never`, `activeDeadlineSeconds` = the longest the session can last, since a deadline cannot be raised and Extend can move the limit (the console ends the session at its limit), `ttlSecondsAfterFinished` = `toolbox.debugPod.ttlAfterFinished`) from a chart profile (image, requests, limits, capabilities), pinned with `nodeName` when opened from a node, with the chosen ServiceAccount, with labels either none, copied from a pod the person picks (minus controller selector labels: `pod-template-hash`, `controller-uid`, `batch.kubernetes.io/controller-uid`, `controller-revision-hash`, `statefulset.kubernetes.io/pod-name`) or typed, plus `221b.io/session`, a readiness gate that never becomes Ready so no Service puts it in its Endpoints, a non-root security context with all capabilities dropped and only the profile's added, seccomp `RuntimeDefault`. The dialog shows *What will be created*; the card shows the server dry run. Then a shell into its container opens. | On kind: a netshoot pod with a pod's labels runs `dig` against a Service; it never appears in Endpoints; it is gone 10 minutes after the session. |
| TB-29 | Ending the session deletes the Job and so the pod; the time limit ends it by the deadline; the TTL removes what is left. | Each path leaves nothing behind; a test lists the namespace after each. |
| TB-30 | The dialog opens from a node's menu (pinned to the node) and from the Namespaces tab's row menu (any node), with the right prefill. | Both entry points open the same dialog. |
| TB-31 | **Node shell** creates a pod in the toolbox namespace (`toolbox.nodeShell.namespace`, by default the release's namespace with `-privileged`, so `221b-privileged` for a console in `221b`; changed at the Phase 10 review, created by the chart at Pod Security `privileged`, TB-49) as the person: `nodeName`, tolerations for every taint, `restartPolicy: Never`, `activeDeadlineSeconds` = the longest the session can last, as for a debug pod (TB-28), the chart's node image, and the profile's security context: **general** (shown as *Restricted*) = `hostPID`, `hostNetwork`, `hostIPC`, the node's root at `/host` (hostPath `/`), not privileged; **netadmin** = `hostNetwork` plus `NET_ADMIN` and `NET_RAW`; **sysadmin** = general plus `privileged: true`. The pane's first lines name the pod, the profile and what it can do; the person runs `chroot /host` themselves. Then a shell session into it opens. The dialog carries the blast-radius callout (root over every pod on the node, Secrets included). | On kind: a general node shell reads `journalctl -u kubelet` through `/host`; sysadmin runs `nsenter -t 1 -m`; the pod is deleted at the end. |

| TB-34 | A node that is NotReady cannot start the pod; the dialog says so before the card and the card's dry run says it again. A cordoned node works. | A cordoned node runs a node shell; a NotReady node is refused with the reason. |

| TB-36 | Where a second person must approve a tool (TB-37), its request is a **Watson card** of a new kind, `session`, beside `read` and `change`: it names the tool, the object, the identity, the duration and the reason; shows what will be created or patched (the pod, Job or ephemeral container as YAML; the exact command for exec) and the server dry run where Kubernetes offers one (pods, Jobs, ephemeral containers), saying plainly that there is none for a shell or a port forward; and states the session terms (recorded or not, idle and hard limits). | A card for each tool matches the mockups; the dry run is shown or its absence explained. |
| TB-37 | A tool needs no approval of its own: the person filled in its dialog and pressed its button, and nothing in it was written unseen by a model (changed at the October 26, 2026 review). | TB-38 | A session card lapses after `Lapse` (10 minutes) like other cards, or 30 minutes while it waits for a second person; a lapsed request shows `lapsed` in its dock tab and in History. The approvers' Watson pane shows requests waiting on them with a badge on the Assistants tab, and the Sessions tab shows `N waiting`. | Lapse and badges are tested against the simulator. |
| TB-39 | Nothing from a Tools menu reaches the cluster but the fixed request its dialog or item makes, as the person: the session endpoint opens a session at once or makes the card of TB-36; the menu's changes (**Cordon**, **Uncordon**, **Drain**, **Delete** a pod) go to `POST …/watson/actions` as an action and a name, never as text, and the server writes the kubectl line, runs it in Watson as the person without a card to approve (on a production connection it waits for a second person) and keeps it in Watson's list and the audit log. | A request for an action outside the four, or with a name that is not a Kubernetes name, is refused; Cordon runs with no card to approve. |
| TB-40 | A read-mode connection (`localCluster.mode: read`, or a connection in read mode) refuses every tool before the dialog, and the menu items say `off: this connection is read-only`. | TB-41 | Beside a terminal, Sherlock (when on) answers about the session: *Ask about this output* sends the selected lines (4,000 characters at most, scrubbed like every tool result, ADR-44) with the question; Sherlock's suggested commands are chips that place the command at the prompt (TB-18). Sherlock never types into a session and sees no more of the transcript than the person selects. | A question about a selected line is answered about it; the request the fake API receives holds only the selection. |
| TB-42 | A new UI action, **`open_tool_dialog`** (tool, cluster, namespace, pod or node, container, image profile, a drafted reason), joins the registry of docs/sherlock-ui-control.md: it opens the tool's dialog, prefilled and marked `Prefilled by Sherlock from <what>`; the person edits and presses the primary button, which opens the session (TB-37). Run a command and Run a command on the node are not among its tools. The action runs only when the target exists and the person may see it (the same access-filtered lookup as clicking, P6U-07) and only for a tool the person may use (TB-05). **P6U-12 is amended**: this one dialog may be opened; no action approves a card, starts a session or types into one. | "I need to look inside gateway" yields a chip; the chip opens the dialog prefilled; a target the person may not see yields "not available" and no dialog; the P6U-04 contract test lists the action. |
| TB-43 | Sherlock picks the tool: a pod whose image has no shell gets a debug-container suggestion; a node problem a node command or a node shell; a network question a debug pod with the right labels; the drafted reason cites the alert or finding it came from. | The evaluation set (P5-06) gains three toolbox cases. |
| TB-44 | **Administration › Sessions**: Live and History, per cluster, every session with started, type, target, namespace, person, approved by, duration, ended by (`N min idle`, `time limit`, `the person`, `<admin> (admin)` with the reason, `pod deleted`, `console restarted`, `refused by <name>`, `lapsed`), and the record (transcript size, output size, connection count); filters by type, person, ending and range; a row expands to the session's audit trail; Export as on the other tables. SuperAdmins see everything; others their own. | Matches the mockup; the trail of a session lists request, approval, open, transfers, watchers and end. |
| TB-45 | Session records (one per session, no transcript) are kept in a ConfigMap `221b-sessions` in the console's namespace, newest first, capped at `toolbox.history.keep` (default 2,000); the audit log and webhook are the durable record. | 2,000 records fit under 1 MiB; the oldest is dropped at the cap. |
| TB-46 | **Administration › Toolbox**: read-only, per cluster, generated from the chart values and the connection: each tool on or off and who may use it, the limits, the approval rule in force, the allowed images with pinned digests, tools and added capabilities, the toolbox namespace, the CLI version, and the `values.yaml` it came from; a check line says whether the live toolbox namespace and the roles match the chart (as the Permissions page does for roles, RB-36). | Matches the mockup; a hand-edited namespace label shows as differing. |
| TB-47 | The Permissions page (RB-44) gains rows for `pods/exec`, `pods/portforward`, `pods/ephemeralcontainers`, `jobs`, and `pods` in the toolbox namespace, flagged Sensitive. | The rows match the deployed roles. |
| TB-48 | A `toolbox` block in `values.yaml` (below): the ceiling the console never exceeds. Per connection, a `toolbox` override in the connection's settings can only turn tools off or lower limits. | `helm template` renders the namespace, the Role and the NetworkPolicy; an override that raises a limit is refused with a message. |
| TB-49 | The chart creates the toolbox namespace with `pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: privileged` (and `warn`, `audit`), a NetworkPolicy that allows its pods nothing but the API server, and a label naming the console as its owner so admission policies can except it. The read-only templates gain nothing. Who may use a tool follows from the templates, not from the toolbox values: Shell for SuperAdmin and, with the switch, Admin; Port forward for SuperAdmin and Admin; Debug container for SuperAdmin; Debug pod for SuperAdmin and Admin in bound namespaces; Node shell for SuperAdmin. | `hack/check-rbac.sh` and the RB-56 role tests pass; a Viewer's `auth can-i --list` shows no new verb. |
| TB-51 | Everything runs through impersonation (P3-02): exec, port-forward, the ephemeral-container patch, the Job and the node pod are created as the person, and Kubernetes' refusal is shown with its own message. The console never uses its ServiceAccount for a session. | A person whose role lacks `pods/exec` sees Kubernetes' Forbidden in the pane; the API server audit shows the impersonated user. |
| TB-52 | Admission: the dialog states the namespace's Pod Security level and disables the profiles above it; the card's dry run catches the rest (webhooks, quotas, LimitRanges). | A deny from a validating webhook in the dry run is shown on the card. |
| TB-53 | Audit records (P3-10) `session.request`, `session.approve`, `session.refuse`, `session.lapse`, `session.open`, `session.watch`, `session.transfer`, `session.extend`, `session.end`, `session.transcript` (a transcript downloaded), each with the session id, tool, cluster, target, identity, approver, reason, duration, ended-by and byte counts, and for one command the command and the size of its output. One record per event, to standard output and the webhook like every other. | A shell session writes request, approve, open and end records; a one-command session's end record holds its command and the size of its output, not the output. |
| TB-54 | **Transcripts.** `toolbox.transcripts`: `none`, `pvc` or `s3`. With `pvc` the chart creates a volume (`size`, `storageClass`; RWX, or `replicaCount: 1`); with `s3` an endpoint, a bucket and a Secret; with `none` (the default) no transcript is kept, the Transcript buttons are absent, and the Toolbox page says so. A transcript is asciicast v2 (keystrokes, output, timings), kept for `toolbox.retention` (default 400 days), downloadable by SuperAdmins and the session's owner, and treated as sensitive: it may hold anything typed, Secrets included. Port forwards keep connections and bytes only. | With `pvc` on kind a shell's transcript replays in `asciinema`; with `none` nothing is written and the UI says so. |
| TB-55 | **Watch**: a SuperAdmin can attach read-only to a live terminal session from the Sessions pane; the owner's pane shows `<name> is watching`, and the watcher is in the audit trail. Reading the output of someone else's live session through `GET sessions/{id}/output` counts as watching: it is audited, and the owner sees the name for 30 seconds after the read. Reading an ended session's output is audited too. | Two browsers on the simulator: the watcher sees output and cannot type; the owner sees the pill. |
| TB-56 | The simulator (`221b-sim`) gains a fake exec and port-forward (an echo shell, a distroless pod that refuses exec, a crash-looping pod, a node at 84% disk) so the dialogs, the Dock, the approval flow and the Sessions pages run without a cluster, and `fakeclaude` answers the Sherlock cases of TB-43. | The screenshots in `docs/screenshots/` come from the simulator and match the mockups. |
| TB-57 | Unit tests: the TB-05 matrix, session lifetimes and every end cause, the card kind and both approval paths, the Job and node-pod specs for each profile, label copying and the readiness gate, the tunnel framing, the audit records. | CI passes. |
| TB-58 | e2e on kind: shell, one command, a file download, a port forward through the CLI, a debug container on a distroless image, a debug pod with copied labels, a node shell with each profile, the two-person path with two signed-in people, an idle end, an admin's End, a console restart. | CI passes; the RB-56 role tests are extended with the new kinds. |
| TB-59 | Performance: 50 live sessions per replica with no visible lag, under 50 ms added per keystroke on the test cluster, 20 MB/s through a forward, server memory within the P1-18 budget with sessions open. | Measured against kind and the simulator. |

## Phase 10: Helm applications and Secret editing

A Helm page (shown as **Helm1** beside the Helm tab until it replaces it) that lists every release in one table and installs, upgrades and removes releases from a chart in a git repository, at a branch or a tag; and Secret editing on the Namespaces tab for Admins and SuperAdmins, with values shown only where the chart allows it. [helm-apps.md](helm-apps.md) has the approach, the chart values and what was built; the rows below are its requirements. Asked for on October 27, 2026.

| ID | Requirement | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HA-01 | The **Helm** tab (built as Helm1 beside the old tab, which it replaced at the October 28, 2026 review; the address `#/helm/<namespace>/<release>` is unchanged) lists every release the person may see in one table, with no summary above it: Name, Namespace, Chart, App version, Revision, Status, Drift, Source and Updated, with the status segments and *Show cluster add-ons*. Search, the Columns menu and Export work as on the other tables. The tab needs Secrets. | The table lists what `helm list -A` lists; a release with no source shows `—` under Drift and Source. |
| HA-02 | Only an Admin or a SuperAdmin may create, upgrade, switch, change, inspect with its saved token or remove an application, and only in a namespace where Kubernetes lets them list and create Secrets, which is asked as the person before the record is read (the console's own account writes the record, so Kubernetes would not see it). `--app` on a Watson line holds to the same, for the person who asks and the person who runs it. Everyone sees the table and a release's details. Kubernetes decides each change, since it runs as the person (HA-06). | A Viewer sees no New application button and no tools; the API answers 403. An Admin of one namespace gets the same 403 for another's application whether a record is kept or not. |
| HA-03 | What the console keeps about an application is a record in a Secret of its own namespace, beside the cluster connections: the source (repository, chart path, branch or tag), the commit last deployed with its date and first line, the chart version there, the values file, the parameters, and a token for a private repository, which never leaves the server. With a database the record is a row of `applications` (DB-62), and the Secret keeps what people type into it (the values file, the parameters, the commands' parameters) and the token; an application with none of them has no Secret. | The record survives a restart; `helm get values` of the console shows no token; the audit log names keys, never a token or a value. |
| HA-04 | The console fetches the chart itself: a shallow clone of the repository at the ref, in memory (go-git; the image has no git), honouring `.helmignore`. A fetch is bounded: 64 MB sent, 200,000 objects, 128 MB unpacked, and 128 MB and 20,000 files and directories at the ref, each refused in plain words; two run at once. A chart path holds letters, digits and `. _ + @ -`, in segments that do not start with a dash. The console does not connect to loopback, link-local or cloud metadata addresses: the name is checked when a source is saved, and the address at every connection. Private addresses stay allowed, for on-premises git servers. A chart whose dependencies are not vendored under `charts/` is refused with a message: the console fetches no dependencies from chart repositories (charts themselves come from Helm repositories and OCI registries too since wave 5, HR-01). A chart runs inside the console, so a render has a timeout of 60 seconds and at most 32 MB of output, four run at once, and `until`, `untilStep`, `seq` and `repeat` spend a budget. | A chart in a subdirectory of a public GitHub repository renders; a missing directory, a missing Chart.yaml and a missing ref each give a plain message; so do a repository past a limit and a chart that loops without end. |
| HA-05 | **New application…** asks for the namespace (an existing one, or a new one with *Create the namespace*), the release name, the repository URL, the chart path, the branch or tag (listed from the repository on request, or typed), an optional token, a values file and parameters, then installs. | The dialog lists the repository's branches and tags; the install appears in Watson and the release in the table with its source. |
| HA-06 | Every change is a Watson command that runs as the person: `helm install`, `helm upgrade` and `helm uninstall` gain `--git`, `--path`, `--ref`, `--tag` and `--app` (the saved values and parameters). Each is a card the person reads and approves in Watson; on a production cluster with the two-person rule a second person approves, as for any change. | The audit log records the command with its source; the card shows a dry run. A Helm dry run renders on the console, whatever `--dry-run` says: `lookup` finds nothing, Secret objects are left out and values that look like credentials are masked, in any kind, so its output holds no Secret value of the cluster. |
| HA-07 | The card of an install or upgrade says the chart, its version and app version, the source and commit, the objects it creates, changes or deletes against what is deployed, and the images it runs; an uninstall's card lists what it deletes. The card pins the commit and the values it used: the run deploys that commit, by its id when the ref has moved, and refuses in plain words if the commit cannot be had or the values changed. Like every card, it is read as the person who asked: what they may not read is left out, and the card says so. | The card's impact names the objects and images for the seeded chart; a push to the branch after the card changes nothing that is deployed; an edit of the saved values makes the run refuse. |
| HA-08 | After a successful install or upgrade the record carries the commit deployed, its date and subject, and the chart version at it; after an uninstall the record is removed. A record whose install failed stays in the table as *not installed* (asked at the October 27, 2026 review), with Install, its values, Change source and Forget; a new application of the same name replaces it. Removing one that has no Helm release (never installed, or its release removed outside the console) removes its record: the uninstall says *there is no release "x": the application's record was removed* and succeeds, and its card says the console forgets it (0.13.0; the ninth cluster test found the uninstall failed and the row stayed). | The Source column and the overview show the commit; an uninstalled release leaves no record; a failed install leaves a row that can be retried or forgotten. |
| HA-09 | **Drift**: each object of the release's manifest is read from the cluster, as the person, and what the manifest sets is compared with what is there; a field set differently or an object missing is drift, what the cluster added (defaults, status, Helm's labels) is not, hooks are skipped, and values are never shown. The check runs when a release is opened and on *Check now*, and its answer is kept two minutes. The column reads in sync, drifted, unknown or not checked. | A deployment scaled by hand shows `spec.replicas` as drift; the same objects show in sync. |
| HA-10 | A release's **Tools** (in the table and in its panel): Upgrade…, Switch branch or tag…, Values and parameters…, Set source… (or Change source…), Forget source, Uninstall…. A release without a source offers Set source and Uninstall. | Each item opens its dialog or confirmation; a Viewer sees none. |
| HA-11 | A release the console did not install can be adopted: Set source… keeps its repository, path and ref (and deploys from them at once when asked, with `helm upgrade --install`). Forget source drops the record and keeps the release. | A seeded release gains a source and then upgrades from it. |
| HA-12 | A **values file** (YAML, as `helm -f`) is kept per application and edited in place on the Values tab, in a large editor (60% of the window, Tab indents), with *Save for the next upgrade* and *Save and upgrade*; it applies over the chart's defaults. | The values file is edited above the values as deployed; an upgrade applies it. |
| HA-13 | **Parameters**, one `key=value` per line as `helm --set` takes them, apply over the values file. | A parameter overrides the same key of the values file in the rendered manifest. |
| HA-14 | Clicking a release folds the table into the Helm tab's side list (name, status, chart, pods ready, namespace, revision, age; the status segments and *Show cluster add-ons*) and opens the release's panel on the right: everything the Helm tab shows (the failed and not-ready callouts, the CPU, memory, pods and storage cards, Pods, Workloads, Storage, Network, Roles, Other, Values, Notes, History) with an Overview first (source, commit, drift) and Images last; a Tools button; a cross closes it (reworded at the October 27, 2026 review). | The URL is `#/helm1/<namespace>/<release>?tab=`; the cross returns to the full table; the sections match the Helm tab's for the same release. |
| HA-15 | **Images**: every image a revision's manifest names, with its tag or digest, registry and the object and container that use it; any revision can be picked. | The seeded chart's images list with their tags; a revision without containers says so. |
| HA-16 | **History** lists the revisions (updated, status, description, chart, objects) and says when a newer version exists: a newer tag of the same major version when a tag is deployed, or new commits on the deployed branch, with a way to switch or upgrade. | A repository with a newer tag shows it; a branch that moved shows the head commit. |
| HA-17 | **Diff**: each revision in the History table has a wrench whose menu offers **Diff…** (and the rollback of M-03; icons until the Phase 10.1 review), which opens a dialog large enough to read (96% of the window), with that revision against the one before it, and a choice of any two revisions or of the deployed revision against what the source renders, at the deployed ref or another (reworked at the October 28, 2026 review). The source side is a server dry run of the upgrade as the person, so the chart's lookups, the cluster's capabilities and the next revision are the real ones; without that right it renders on the console and the label says so. A Secret's values show as their sizes, never the values. | Two seeded revisions diff object by object; the same manifests say so; a Secret's value is not in the text. |
| HA-18 | Sherlock's `view_open` opens the tab with a release's path, as before. The old Helm tab was removed once this page showed everything it did (October 28, 2026). | `view_open` with tab `helm` and a path opens the release. |
| HA-19 | Every read the page makes (revisions, manifests, images, drift) is made as the person; the console's own account reads nothing of a release for the page. Looking the repository over with the saved token is for those who manage the application (HA-02). | A person without access to a namespace gets 403 from each endpoint. |
| HA-20 | Tests: Go tests for the fetch, the render, the images, the diff and the drift against a local repository, and for the API with a fake cluster; browser tests against the simulator for the table, the panel and the Secret editing, and on the kind cluster an install from a public repository. | CI passes. |
| SE-01 | A chart value `secrets.reveal` (off by default, needs `secrets.read`) says whether a SuperAdmin, or an Admin of the Secret's namespace, may see a Secret's values. | `helm template` refuses `secrets.reveal=true` with `secrets.read=false`. |
| SE-02 | An Admin or a SuperAdmin may edit a Secret on the Namespaces tab: replace a key's value, add a key, remove one, each written as them, so Kubernetes decides; a Viewer or SuperViewer may not. With revealing off the new values are typed blind (a *Show what I type* box shows the typing). | The seeded Secret's value changes in the cluster; a Viewer sees no Edit button and gets 403. |
| SE-03 | With `secrets.reveal` on, a key gains Reveal: one value at a time, read live as the person, never cached, and recorded in the audit log with the key's name; binary values show as base64. Kubernetes is asked, as the person, for the right to write Secrets in that namespace, which Admin has and Viewer has not, so Admin held elsewhere does not count (RB-06); a read-only Admin cannot be told from a Viewer and is refused. With it off the server answers 403 and says why. | The audit log has `secret.reveal` with the key; the planted value still reaches no snapshot, export or log. (Amended on November 9, 2026, BK-15: a revealed value is a cluster's Secret and is never in a backup; what a backup carries sealed is only what the console keeps of its own, X-01.) |
| SE-04 | An edit names only the keys in the audit log, is refused when the Secret changed since it was read (when the page gives the version it read), and refuses an immutable Secret. | `secret.update` carries `set=` and `removed=`; a stale edit answers 409. |
| SE-05 | **New Secret…** on a namespace's Secrets tab makes a Secret with a name, a type (Opaque by default) and keys, written as the person. A namespace with no Secret still shows its Secrets tab to an Admin, so the first one can be made. | The Secret appears in the namespace's list with its keys and sizes. |
| NS-02 | A namespace's wrench (beside its name) offers **Delete namespace…**: after a confirmation, `kubectl delete namespace NAME` goes to Watson as a card whose impact says what goes with it (pods, claims and their volumes' fate, Secrets, ConfigMaps, Helm releases); it runs as the approver. | The card lists what is in the namespace; after the approval the namespace is gone. |
| NS-01 | **New namespace…** on the Namespaces tab (and on the wrench) makes a namespace with a name and optional labels, written as the person, so Kubernetes decides; the tab then opens it. | The namespace appears in the list; a name that is not a DNS label is refused; the audit log has `namespace.create`. |
| SE-06 | X-01 still holds everywhere else: the cache, exports, logs, the YAML view and Sherlock carry no value; CI's planted value test covers the new endpoints. | The planted-value test passes with the endpoints present. |

### Your review

- [ ] An install from GitHub (HA-04 to HA-08), an upgrade to another tag, a switch back, a values change and an uninstall, each in Watson and the audit log.
- [ ] The History tab's newer-version notice and the diffs (HA-16, HA-17), and the Images tab (HA-15).
